Stability Conditions for 3 3 -fold Flops Thanks: YH was supported by JSPS KAKENHI 19K14502 and The Max Planck Institute for Mathematics, and MW by EPSRC grants EP/R009325/1 and EP/R034826/1.
Yuki Hirano, Michael Wemyss

Abstract
. Let f : X → Spec ⁡ R f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R be a 3 3 -fold flopping contraction, where X X has at worst Gorenstein terminal singularities and R R is complete local. We describe the space of Bridgeland stability conditions on the null subcategory 𝒞 \mathcal{C} of D b ⁡ ( coh ⁡ X ) \mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X) , which consists of those complexes that derive pushforward to zero, and also on the affine subcategory 𝒟 \mathcal{D} , which consists of complexes supported on the exceptional locus. We show that a connected component Stab ∘ ​ 𝒞 \mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt{\mathcal{C}} of Stab ⁡ 𝒞 \operatorname{Stab}{\mathcal{C}} is the universal cover of the complexified complement of the real hyperplane arrangement associated to X X via the Homological MMP, and more generally that Stab n ∘ ​ 𝒟 \mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D} is a regular covering space of the infinite hyperplane arrangement constructed in [ IW2 ] . Neither arrangement is Coxeter in general. As a consequence, we give the first description of the Stringy Kähler Moduli Space (SKMS) for all smooth irreducible 3 3 -fold flops. The answer is surprising: we prove that the SKMS is always a sphere, minus either 3 , 4 , 6 , 8 , 12 3,4,6,8,12 or 14 14 points, depending on the length of the curve.



1.
Introduction

Our setting is
3
-fold flopping contractions, namely
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, where
(R,\mathfrak{m})
is a three-dimensional complete local Gorenstein
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with at worst terminal singularities. We allow
X
to be singular, with
X
having at worst terminal singularities. Consider the fibre
\mathrm{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f^{-1}(\mathfrak{m})
, which with its reduced scheme structure is well-known to decompose into a union of
n
irreducible curves, each isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Given this setup, consider the following two subcategories of
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathcal{F}\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\mid\mathbf{R}f_{*}\mathcal{F}=0\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathcal{F}\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\mid\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{C}\}.

It is a fundamental question to describe the spaces of stability conditions on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, and to use this to help describe the autoequivalence group of
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
. Both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
have finite length hearts, and it is well-known from surfaces
[
B6
]
that stability conditions on
\mathcal{C}
should exhibit ‘finite-type’ ADE behaviour, whilst
\mathcal{D}
should be the ‘affine’ version.

One of the problems is that traditional finite and affine Coxeter groups do not suffice in this setting. On one hand, it is possible that
\mathcal{C}
is controlled by a Coxeter arrangement that does not have an associated affine Coxeter group. On the other hand, the category
\mathcal{D}
predicts that such an affine object ‘exists’. Even worse, it is possible that
\mathcal{C}
is controlled by a simplicial hyperplane arrangement that is not Coxeter. In that case, the affine object that controls
\mathcal{D}
is even less clear. Making precise statements about both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
is in fact one of the main outcomes of this paper.

1.1.
Hidden t-structures

Our approach to this problem is noncommutative, and necessarily so. One of our new insights is that many of the t-structures that arise in the stability manifold of
\mathcal{D}
are ‘hidden’, in the sense that they are not obviously part of the birational geometry, nor are they translations of the birational geometry by line bundle twists. However, they do have very conceptual noncommutative interpretations. To describe this in more detail, it is helpful to first briefly review the known cases.

The first partial solution to describing stability conditions on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
in this
3
-fold setting is due to Toda
[
T
]
, who worked under two additional assumptions: (1)
X
is smooth, and (2) for a generic hyperplane section
H\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}R
, the pullback
X\times_{R}H
is smooth. Both conditions are restrictive for different reasons, with the second being the least natural, and by far the most problematic to remove. The crucial point is that, under these additional assumptions, the dual graph is an ADE Dynkin diagram. When this happens, the traditional language of finite and affine Weyl groups suffice, and the relevant t-structures are all described by perverse sheaves and their tensors by line bundles. Toda
[
T
]
packages this together to describe a component of normalised stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
as a regular covering of the complexified complement of the associated affine root hyperplane arrangement. Furthermore, the Galois group has a very satisfying geometric description, as those compositions of flop functors and line bundle twists that act trivially on K-theory.

Alas, these satisfyingly geometric statements all fail without assumption (2). Perhaps counter-intuitively, the hardest case turns out to be the most elementary one: that of a single-curve flop. In this case, the flopping curve has an associated
length
invariant
\ell
, which is some number between one and six. The assumption (2) holds if and only if the curve has length one. Evidently, this is quite restrictive.

One of our key observations is that, in the general situation of a 3-fold flop
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, tracking under flop functors and line bundle twists does not suffice. To illustrate this visually in the case of a two curve flop, we will show below that stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
are controlled by infinite hyperplane arrangements
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
such as that shown in Figure
1
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[angle,scale]{E8example.pdf}\end{array}

Figure 1.
Example of hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}

In general the hyperplane arrangements are quite complicated, and there are many more chambers than one might naively expect. The above example has an obvious
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
action, given by tensoring by the line bundles corresponding to the two curves. However, this action jumps the central chamber over many intermediate t-structures. These all turn out to be hearts of noncommutative resolutions, and their variants.

To circumvent this problem, which occurs even in the case of a single curve flop, we appeal to recent advances in noncommutative resolutions and their mutation theory. In the process, we will recover a conceptual understanding of the hidden t-structures, give a full description of (a component of) normalised stability conditions on
\mathcal{D}
, and for the first time compute the Stringy Kähler Moduli Space.

1.2.
Main Stability Results

Describing stability conditions on
\mathcal{C}
turns out to be quite easy. Working in our most general setup
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, we first prove the following, which is entirely parallel to
[
T
, Section 6, ArXiv v2]
. Below the hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
need not be ADE, or even Coxeter, but nevertheless tracking under the Flop functors still produces the chambers of the stability manifold for the category
\mathcal{C}
. As notation, consider the set
\operatorname{Flop}(X)
consisting of all those pairs
(F,Y)
where
Y\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is obtained from
X
through an iterated chain of simple flops, and
F
is a composition of flop functors and their inverses, from
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}Y)
to
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
.

Theorem 1.1

(
6.4
,
6.9
)
.

There is a union of chambers

\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}=\bigcup_{(Y,F)\in\,\operatorname{Flop}(X)}F(U),

where
U
is defined in Notation
6.1
, and furthermore the natural map

\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\\

is the universal cover of the complexified complement of
\mathcal{H}
.

The hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
can be described in various ways, and this is explained in Section
3
. The main content of the above theorem is to establish that the map is a regular covering map; our previous work
[
HW
]
on the faithfulness of the action then establishes that the cover is universal. From this, the seminal work of Deligne
[
D1
]
on the
K(\uppi,1)
conjecture for simplicial hyperplane arrangements immediately confirms the following.

Corollary 1.2

(
6.10
)
.

\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
is contractible.

However, the main content in this paper is our description of stability conditions on the category
\mathcal{D}
, which is much harder. Passing to a more noncommutative viewpoint, by the HomMMP
[
W1
, 4.2]
we first observe that the union in Theorem
1.1
can be reindexed using instead those pairs
(\Upphi,L)
where
\Upphi
is a chain of mutation functors and their inverses, and
L
belongs to the mutation class
\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
of
N
described in (
2.C
), where mutation at the submodule
R
is
not
allowed. Disregarding this last restriction, and thus allowing mutation at all summands, gives an infinite set
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
. Via
[
IW2
]
, this turns out to index the chambers in a corresponding infinite hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

The following is our main result. The Galois group
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
is by definition all compositions of mutation functors and their inverses that start and finish at our fixed
\operatorname{End}_{R}(N)
.

Theorem 1.3

(
6.4
,
6.9
)
.

There is a union of chambers

\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{(\Upphi,L)}\Upphi(\mathds{N}_{L}).

where
\mathds{N}_{L}
is defined in Notation
6.1
, and
\Upphi
are compositions of mutation functors and their inverses. Furthermore, the natural forgetful map

\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}\\

is a regular covering map, with Galois group
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
.

Whilst passing to noncommutative resolutions (and their variants) provides the conceptual framework to tackle the above problem, and to understand the extra t-structures, their appearance comes at a significant cost. Namely, it becomes much harder to argue when functors are the identity, and thus to establish that
\mathcal{Z}
is a regular covering map. The following is one of our main technical results, which heavily uses the isolated cDV assumption.

Theorem 1.4

(
5.11
)
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
is an arbitrary modifying algebra (or noncommutative crepant resolution) of
R
, where
R
is isolated cDV. Consider an equivalence

G\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)

obtained as an arbitrarily long sequence of mutation functors and their inverses. If
G
restricts to an equivalence
\operatorname{mod}\Gamma\to\operatorname{mod}\Gamma
, then
G\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.

There are additional variants to the above, summarised in Theorem
5.6
, which may be of independent interest.

1.3.
Autoequivalence and SKMS results

Aside from producing new invariants for
3
-fold flops, linking to classification problems, autoequivalences, noncommutative resolutions and deformation theory, one of our main motivations for establishing Theorem
1.3
is that it provides the first mechanism to compute the fabled stringy Kähler moduli space (SKMS). To do this requires some additional work, since following and generalising
[
T
]
we view the SKMS as the quotient of
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
by a certain group
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
.

The definition of the group
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
is a rather subtle point, since in this local model everything is relative to the base
\operatorname{Spec}R
, and so everything should respect this structure. In particular,
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
should not contain isomorphisms between flopping contractions unless they preserve the
R
-scheme structure. We achieve this by defining
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
to be the group of
R
-linear
Fourier–Mukai equivalences
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
that preserve
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
. Even for a single curve flop with
\ell=1
, the restriction to
R
-linear functors is necessary for the mathematically defined SKMS
[
T
, p6169]
to coincide with the physical version
[
A
, Figure 1]
.

The intrinsically defined group
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
has the following more concrete description.

Theorem 1.5

(
7.10
)
.

\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cong\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\rtimes\operatorname{Pic}X
.

Combining Theorem
1.3
with Proposition
1.5
and an elementary hyperplane calculation allows us to finally compute the SKMS, as
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}/\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
, for smooth irreducible flops, generalising
[
T
, p6169]
and
[
A
, Figure 1]
. There does not appear to be any predictions or conjectures in the literature for what the SKMS should be for higher lengths. Perhaps this is just as well, since the result is quite surprising.

Corollary 1.6

(
7.12
)
.

For a smooth irreducible flop
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
of length
\ell
, the SKMS is always a
2
-sphere, with holes removed at both the north and south pole, together with the following number of holes removed from the equator.

\ell

1

2

3

4

5

6

Holes

1

2

4

6

10

12

For example, when
\ell=4
, it follows that the SKMS is

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 4
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 4

where we refer the reader to Theorem
7.12
for more details, including an explanation of the numerics, and the labelling of the holes on the equator.

List of Notation

A list of notation is provided in Appendix
B
.

2.
Flops via Noncommutative Methods

In this section, we recall the basics of modification algebras, tilting, mutations of modifying modules, and the relationship to flops, mainly to set notation. Throughout
R
is a three dimensional complete local Gorenstein normal
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, and
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is a flopping contraction as in the introduction. Furthermore, write
\mathrm{C}
for
f^{-1}(\mathfrak{m})
endowed with reduced scheme structure. It is well known that
\mathrm{C}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{C}_{i}
is a union of
n

\mathbb{P}^{1}
s.

2.1.
Tilting and Modification Modules

Since
R
is complete local, there exist line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)
such that
\mathcal{L}_{i}\cdot\mathrm{C}_{j}=\updelta_{ij}
. Set
\mathcal{V}_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}_{X}
, and write
\mathcal{V}_{i}
for the vector bundle arising as the universal extension

(2.A)

0\to\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\oplus r_{i}-1}\to\mathcal{V}_{i}\to\mathcal{L}_{i}\to 0,

associated to a minimal set of
r_{i}-1
generators of the
R
-module
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathcal{L}_{i}^{*})
. Then by
[
VdB
, 3.5.5]
the vector bundle
\mathcal{V}_{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}^{*}
is tilting, and so after setting

\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X})\cong\operatorname{End}_{R}(f_{*}\mathcal{V}_{X}),

the functor

(2.B)

\Uppsi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X},-)\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)

is an equivalence. Our approach to stability conditions will be through noncommutative methods. Recall that
\operatorname{CM}R
denotes the category of (maximal) Cohen–Macaulay
R
-modules, and
\operatorname{ref}R
denotes the category of reflexive
R
-modules. A reflexive
R
-module
L\in\operatorname{ref}R
is called
modifying
if
\operatorname{End}_{R}(L)\in\operatorname{CM}R
.

In the flops setting, for the fixed
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, consider the underived direct image
N_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{*}(\mathcal{V}_{i}^{*})\in\operatorname{mod}R
. Note that
N_{0}\cong R
. Throughout, we set

(2.C)

\displaystyle N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{*}({\mathcal{V}_{X}})\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}N_{i}.

It is known that
N\in\operatorname{CM}R
, and
N
is a modifying
R
-module
[
VdB
, 3.2.10]
.

2.2.
Mutations and Equivalences

Given any modifying
R
-module
L=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{n}L_{j}
with each
L_{j}
indecomposable, there is an operation, called
mutation at
L_{i}
, that gives a new modifying
R
-module written
\upnu_{i}L
. We briefly recall the construction here. Set

L_{i}^{c}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{j\neq i}L_{j},

so that
L=L_{i}\oplus L_{i}^{c}
, and consider the
minimal right
\operatorname{add}(L_{i}^{c})^{*}
-approximation

(2.D)

\displaystyle a_{i}\colon U_{i}\to L_{i}^{*}

of
L_{i}^{*}
, which by definition means that

(1)

U_{i}\in\operatorname{add}(L_{i}^{c})^{*}
and
a_{i}\circ(-)\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{R}((L_{i}^{c})^{*},U_{i})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{R}((L_{i}^{c})^{*},L_{i}^{*})
is surjective,

(2)

If
b\in\operatorname{End}_{R}(U_{i})
satisfies
a_{i}=a_{i}\circ b
, then
b
is an isomorphism.

Since
R
is complete, such an
a_{i}
exists and is unique up to isomorphism. The (left) mutation of
L
at
L_{i}
is then defined to be

\upnu_{i}L\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\operatorname{Ker}a_{i})^{*}\oplus L_{i}^{c}.

The following properties are known.

Proposition 2.1
.

With notation as above, in particular
R
is isolated cDV, the following statements hold.

(1)

The mutation
\upnu_{i}L
is a modifying
R
-module.

(2)

There is an isomorphism
\upnu_{i}\upnu_{i}L\cong L
.

Proof.

The first part is general; see e.g.
[
IW1
, §6]
. The second part is specific to isolated cDV singularities
[
IW2
, 9.28]
.
∎

Definition 2.2
.

Fix the modifying module
N
from (
2.C
). Write
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
for the set of isomorphism classes of all iterated mutations of
N
, and
\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
for the subset consisting of those iterated mutations of
N
at only the
(i\neq 0)
-th summands. The exchange graph
\mathop{\sf EG}\nolimits(N)
is the graph whose vertices are the elements of
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, and two vertices in
\mathop{\sf EG}\nolimits(N)
are joined by an edge if and only if the corresponding modifying modules are related by a mutation at an indecomposable summand. The exchange graph
\mathop{\sf EG}\nolimits_{0}(N)
is the full subgraph whose vertices are the elements of
\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
.

Alternatively, the exchange graph
\mathop{\sf EG}\nolimits_{0}(N)
is the full subgraph whose vertices correspond to CM modules. We once and for all fix a decomposition
N=R\oplus N_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus N_{n}
, where
N_{0}=R
. Via the Coxeter-style combinatorics in Section
3
, this fixed decomposition induces an ordering on the summands of all other elements
L
of
\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, such that locally crossing a wall locally labelled
s_{i}
always corresponds to replacing the
i
th summand. In this way, there is a global labelling on the edges of both
\mathop{\sf EG}\nolimits_{0}(N)
and
\mathop{\sf EG}\nolimits(N)
using the sets
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
and
\{s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
respectively.

The mutation of a modifying
R
-module
L
gives rise to a derived equivalence between
\Gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{R}(L)
and
\upnu_{i}\Gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{R}(\upnu_{i}L)
, induced by a tilting bimodule
T_{i}
. Since
R
is isolated, in fact
T_{i}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,\upnu_{i}L)
by
[
IW1
, 6.14]
, and the following functor is an equivalence:

(2.E)

\displaystyle\Upphi_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\Gamma}(T_{i},-)\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\upnu_{i}\Gamma).

The functor
\Upphi_{i}
is called the
mutation functor
at the summand
i
.

2.3.
Flops and Mutation

Recall that the exceptional locus of
f
, given reduced scheme structure, decomposes into
n
copies of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, namely
\mathrm{C}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{C}_{i}
. For each
\mathrm{C}_{i}
there exists a flopping contraction
g_{i}\colon X\to Y_{i}
which contracts only
\mathrm{C}_{i}
, and the flopping contraction
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
factors through
g_{i}
. Furthermore, there exists a flop
g_{i}^{+}\colon X_{i}^{+}\to Y_{i}
of
g_{i}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}

where
f_{i}^{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}h_{i}\circ g_{i}^{+}
. Then
(f^{+}_{i})^{-1}(\mathfrak{m})
, with reduced scheme structure, is the union of
n
irreducible curves
\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{C}_{j}^{+}
, where for
j\neq i
each
\mathrm{C}_{j}^{+}
is the proper transformation of
\mathrm{C}_{j}
, and if
j=i
then
\mathrm{C}_{i}^{+}
is the flopped curve.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
W1
, 4.2]
)
.

With notation as above, the following hold.

(1)

There is an isomorphism of
R
-modules
\mathrm{H}^{0}(X_{i}^{+},\mathcal{V}_{X_{i}^{+}})\cong\upnu_{i}N
.

(2)

The following diagram of equivalences is functorially commutative

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X_{i}^{+})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\upnu_{i}\Lambda)
\operatorname{\sf Flop}_{i}
\Upphi_{i}
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X},-)
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X_{i}^{+}}(\mathcal{V}_{X_{i}^{+}},-)

where
{\sf Flop}_{i}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X_{i}^{+})
is the quasi-inverse of the Bridgeland–Chen flop functor
[
B3
,
C
]
.

2.4.
R
-linear equivalences

In our flops setup
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, the category
\operatorname{coh}X
is
R
-linear, and thus so too is
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
. Autoequivalences that preserve this structure will be particularly important later. Here we briefly recall the
R
-linear structure, and give some preliminary results.

Since
\operatorname{Spec}R
is an affine scheme, there is a bijection

(2.F)

\operatorname{Mor}(X,\operatorname{Spec}R)\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(R,\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)).

Given
g\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, we will write
\mathsf{g}\colon R\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
for the corresponding morphism.

For
a\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{coh}X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
and
\uplambda\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, consider
\uplambda\cdot a\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{coh}X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
defined by

(\uplambda\cdot a)(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\uplambda|_{U}\cdot a(x)\in\mathcal{G}(U)

for all
x\in\mathcal{F}(U)
. Under this action,
\operatorname{coh}X
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-linear category. The morphism
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
then gives
\operatorname{coh}X
the structure of an
R
-linear category, via
\mathsf{f}\colon R\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
.

The following two results are general, and are not specific to our flops setup. Both are well-known, but for lack of reference we provide the proof.

Proposition 2.4
.

Consider
R
-schemes
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
,
g\colon Y\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, and a morphism
h\colon X\to Y
. Writing
\mathsf{h}\colon\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
for the corresponding morphism, then the following are equivalent.

(1)

g\circ h=f
.

(2)

\mathsf{h}\circ\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{f}
.

(3)

h^{*}\colon\operatorname{coh}Y\to\operatorname{coh}X
is an
R
-linear functor.

If
h
is an isomorphism, the last condition is equivalent to
h_{*}\colon\operatorname{coh}X\to\operatorname{coh}Y
being an
R
-linear functor.

Proof.

Note first that for
y\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)
,
a\in\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
and
x\otimes\uplambda\in h^{*}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}\otimes_{Y}\mathcal{O}_{X}
,

\displaystyle h^{*}(y\cdot a)(x\otimes\uplambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(y\cdot a\otimes 1_{X})(x\otimes\uplambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(y\cdot a(x))\otimes\uplambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(x)\otimes\mathsf{h}(y)\uplambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(y)\cdot(a(x)\otimes\uplambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(y)\cdot\bigl((a\otimes 1_{X})(x\otimes\uplambda)\bigr).

Hence by linearity
h^{*}(y\cdot a)=\mathsf{h}(y)\cdot h^{*}(a)
for all
y\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)
and all
a\in\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2) This is an immediate consequence of the bijection (
2.F
).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3) Assuming (2), then for any
r\in R
and
a\in\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
,

h^{*}(r\cdot a)=h^{*}(\mathsf{g}(r)\cdot a)=\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{g}(r))\cdot h^{*}(g)=\mathsf{f}(r)\cdot h^{*}(g)=r\cdot h^{*}(g),

and so (3) holds.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2) There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{Y}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\mathcal{O}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})\,,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}

where the vertical arrows are
R
-linear isomorphisms. Since
h^{*}
is
R
-linear by assumption, it follows that so too is
\mathsf{h}
. But then

\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{g}(r))=\mathsf{h}(r\cdot 1)=r\cdot\mathsf{h}(1)=r\cdot 1=\mathsf{f}(r),

for all
r\in R
, and thus
\mathsf{h}\circ\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{f}
.

The last statement holds since the inverse of an
R
-linear functor is
R
-linear.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Consider an
R
-scheme
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
, and a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
X
. Then the functor
-\otimes\mathcal{L}\colon\operatorname{coh}X\to\operatorname{coh}X
is
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
-linear, and in particular, is
R
-linear.

Proof.

For any
\uplambda\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
,
a\in\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
and
x\otimes\ell\in\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}
, we have

\bigl((\uplambda\cdot a)\otimes\mathcal{L}\bigr)\bigl(x\otimes\ell\bigr)=\uplambda\cdot a(x)\otimes\ell=(\uplambda\cdot(a\otimes\mathcal{L}))(x\otimes\ell)

and so by linearity the result follows.
∎

3.
Hyperplane Arrangements via
K
-theory

When the generic hyperplane section of
X
is not smooth, it will turn out that stability conditions on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
will not, in general, be the regular covering of a space constructed using global rules. The space will instead be constructed using iterated local rules, which we outline here. This section is largely a summary of
[
IW2
]
suitable for our needs, with the exception of some new results in Sections
3.4
and
3.5
.

3.1.
General Elephants

Slicing the flopping contraction
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
gives rise to combinatorial data, in the form of a labelled ADE Dynkin diagram
\Updelta
, vertices
\Updelta_{\kern 0.40974pt0}
, and a subset
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\Updelta_{\kern 0.40974pt0}
. We briefly recall this here.

Pulling back
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
along the map
\operatorname{Spec}R/g\to\operatorname{Spec}R
for a generic element
g\in R
, gives a morphism
S\to\operatorname{Spec}R/g
, say. By
[
R
]
,
R/g
is an ADE surface singularity, and
S
is a partial crepant resolution. As such,
S
is obtained by blowing down curves in the minimal resolution
\widetilde{S}
, and so by McKay correspondence we can describe
S
combinatorially via a Dynkin diagram
\Updelta
, together with the subset
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\Updelta_{\kern 0.40974pt0}
of those vertices that are blown down to obtain
S
. Thus, by convention,
\mathcal{J}
corresponds to the curves that have been contracted by
\widetilde{S}\to S
.

This data can be extended into the affine setting as follows. Consider the corresponding extended Dynkin diagram
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, and denote the extending vertex by
\star
. Set
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{aff}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{J}
, considered as a subset of the vertices of
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

From this data, consider
\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta|}
and
\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}|}
based by the duals
\upalpha_{i}^{*}
, where the
i
are indexed over the vertices of
\Updelta
(respectively,
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
). Inside these spaces, consider the Weyl chamber
C_{+}
, where all coordinates are positive, and set

\displaystyle{\sf TCone}{(\Updelta)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{w\in W_{\kern-0.73616pt\Updelta}}w(C_{+})

\displaystyle{\sf TCone}{(\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{w\in W_{\kern-0.73616pt\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}}w(C_{+}),

where
W_{\kern-0.81949pt\Updelta}
is the finite Weyl group, and
W_{\kern-0.81949pt\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
the affine Weyl group.

There are subspaces
D_{\mathcal{J}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta|}
and
D_{\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}|}
defined as

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\upvartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta|}\mid\upvartheta_{i}=0\mbox{ if }i\in\mathcal{J}\},

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\upvartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}|}\mid\upvartheta_{i}=0\mbox{ if }i\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{aff}}\}.

These are based by
\upalpha_{i}^{*}
for
i\in\Updelta-\mathcal{J}
, respectively
i\in\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}-\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{aff}}
. As such,
\dim D_{\mathcal{J}}=n
, the number of curves in the flopping contraction, and
\dim D_{\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}=n+1
.

Definition 3.1

(
[
IW2
, §1]
)
.

For
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\Updelta_{\kern 0.40974pt0}
as above,

(1)

{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J})}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\sf TCone}{(\Updelta)}\cap D_{\mathcal{J}}
is called the
\mathcal{J}
-finite hyperplane arrangement
.

(2)

{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\sf TCone}{(\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}\cap D_{\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}

is called the
\mathcal{J}
-affine arrangement
.

3.2.
Affine Hyperplanes via K-theory

The combinatorics of the previous section can also be constructed via K-theory, which is more useful for stability conditions later. Recall from (
2.C
) that the flopping contraction
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
associates a modifying
R
-module
N
, with summands
R=N_{0},N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}
. Set
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{R}(N)
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,N_{i})
, so that
\{\mathcal{P}_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq n}
is the set of all indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-modules. It is well-known that

\mathcal{K}_{N}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}K_{0}(\operatorname{Perf}\Lambda)\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{P}_{i}]\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}.

For every
L\in\operatorname{Mut}N
, this process can be repeated: indeed each
\Lambda_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{R}(L)
has K-theory of the same rank as above, and to avoid confusion write
\mathcal{K}_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}K_{0}(\operatorname{Perf}\Lambda_{L})
. Since the given flopping contraction
f
, and its associated modification algebra
\Lambda
is
fixed
, throughout we will refer to the distinguished object

\mathcal{K}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{K}_{N}.

Every mutation functor
\Upphi_{i}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L})
restricts to an equivalence on perfect complexes, and so write

\upphi_{i}\colon\mathcal{K}_{L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{K}_{\upnu_{i}L}

for the induced isomorphism of
K_{0}
-groups. This map can be represented by an invertible
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrix over
\mathbb{Z}
, which is described as follows.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
L
is modifying, and consider the exchange sequence
[
IW1
, (6.I)]
obtained as the dual of (
2.D
), namely

(3.A)

0\to L_{i}\to\bigoplus_{j\neq i}{L_{j}}^{\oplus b_{ij}}\to K_{i}^{*}.

Write
\mathcal{P}_{j}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,L_{j})
for the projectives in
\Lambda_{L}
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
for the correspondingly ordered projectives in
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
. Then
\upphi_{i}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{K}_{\upnu_{i}L}\to\mathcal{K}_{L}
sends

(3.B)

[\mathcal{Q}_{t}]\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}[\mathcal{P}_{t}]&\mbox{if }t\neq i,\\
-[\mathcal{P}_{i}]+\sum_{j\neq i}b_{ij}[\mathcal{P}_{j}]&\mbox{if }t=i.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Being induced by the tilting bimodule
T_{i}
from (
2.E
), it is clear that
\Upphi_{i}
sends
T_{i}
to
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
. Since
T_{i}
only differs from
\Lambda_{L}
at the summand
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, it is obvious that
\Upphi_{i}
sends
\mathcal{P}_{j}
to
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
whenever
j\neq i
; see e.g.
[
W1
, 4.15(1)]
. Hence
\Upphi_{i}^{-1}
sends
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
to
\mathcal{P}_{j}
.

When
j=i
, under
\Upphi_{i}^{-1}
, the projective
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
gets mapped to the
i
th summand of
T_{i}
, which by definition is
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,K_{i})
. But by
[
IW1
, (6.Q)]
, applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,-)
to (
3.A
) gives an exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,L_{i})\to\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,L_{j})^{\oplus b_{ij}}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,K_{i})\to 0.

From this, the identification in K-theory clearly follows.
∎

For
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, consider the shortest sequence of mutations

L\xrightarrow{i_{1}}\upnu_{i_{1}}L\to\ldots\xrightarrow{i_{n}}N,

and define
\Upphi_{L}
to be the composition of the corresponding mutation functors

(3.C)

\Upphi_{L}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{i_{n}}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{1}}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda).

We write
\upphi_{L}\colon\mathcal{K}_{L}\to\mathcal{K}
for the induced map on K-theory. Throughout, whenever
\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathds{k}
, we will abuse notation and also write
\upphi_{L}\colon\mathcal{K}_{L}\otimes\mathds{k}\to\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathds{k}
. The following result is mainly combinatorial, and it mirrors the corresponding Coxeter statement. We identify the basis element
\upalpha_{i}^{*}\in{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
with
[\mathcal{P}_{i}]\in\mathcal{K}
to allow for the comparison.

Theorem 3.3
.

[
IW2
, 9.8]

Suppose that
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is a
3
-fold flopping contraction, such that
X
has only terminal singularities. Then there is a decomposition

{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}=\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}N}\upphi_{L}(C_{+})\subseteq\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathbb{R}.

In particular, the following statements hold.

(1)

The open decomposition
\bigcup\upphi_{L}(C_{+})
gives the chambers of the
\mathcal{J}
-affine arrangement.

(2)

If
L\ncong M
, then
\upphi_{L}(C_{+})
and
\upphi_{M}(C_{+})
do not intersect.

(3)

\upphi_{L}(C_{+})
and
\upphi_{M}(C_{+})
share a codimension one wall
\iff

L
and
M
differ by the mutation of an indecomposable summand.

We remark that
{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
does not fill
\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}|}
, as can be seen in Example
3.5
below. Because of this, all information is contained in a slice.

Definition 3.4
.

The
real level
is defined to be

\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\biggr\{z\in\mathcal{K}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{R}\Bigm|\sum_{j=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{j})z_{j}=1\biggr\}.

The walls of the open decomposition
\bigcup\upphi_{L}(C_{+})
partition
\operatorname{\sf Level}=\operatorname{\sf Level}_{N}
into open regions

\operatorname{\sf Alcove}_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upphi_{L}(C_{+})\cap\,\operatorname{\sf Level}_{N},

which by Theorem
3.3
are still in bijection with
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
. We call these open regions the
\mathcal{J}
-alcoves, and consider the infinite hyperplane arrangement

(3.D)

\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\sf Level}\backslash\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)}\operatorname{\sf Alcove}_{L}.

Example 3.5
.

Consider
\Updelta=E_{6}
, and
\mathcal{J}
the following choice of unshaded vertices:

Then, either by tracking the
C_{+}
region directly over the labelling set in
[
IW2
, 1.12]
, or by iterating the wall crossing rule in
[
IW2
, 1.20(1)(2)]
, or by intersecting the full Tits cone of affine
E_{6}
with the subspace
\mathbb{R}^{2}
based by the shaded vertex and the extended vertex, it follows that
{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
is the shaded region in the following picture. Further,
\operatorname{\sf Level}
is illustrated by the dotted blue line
\upvartheta_{0}+3\upvartheta_{1}=1
.

\scriptstyle\upvartheta_{1}
\scriptstyle\upvartheta_{0}

The circles on the blue line are, reading top left to bottom right, at
\upvartheta_{1}=1,\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{3},0,-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{1}{2}
. Thus basing
\operatorname{\sf Level}
by
[\mathcal{P}_{1}]
, the level is the infinite hyperplane arrangement

\scriptstyle\upvartheta_{1}
\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle\frac{1}{3}
\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}
\scriptstyle\frac{2}{3}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{3}
\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}
\scriptstyle-\frac{2}{3}
\scriptstyle-1

The
\mathcal{J}
-alcoves are the open intervals on the blue line between two adjacent dots, and
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
is the infinite collection of dots.

3.3.
Finite Hyperplanes by K-theory

For the finite version of the above combinatorics, with notation as in Lemma
3.2
consider

\Uptheta_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{K}_{L}/[\mathcal{P}_{0}]\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Again, since
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
and
N
are fixed from (
2.C
), there is a distinguished object
\Uptheta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Uptheta_{N}
. If
i\neq 0
, then since
\upphi_{i}
sends
\mathcal{P}_{0}
to
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
by Lemma
3.2
,
\upphi_{i}
induces an isomorphism

\upvarphi_{i}\colon\Uptheta_{L}\to\Uptheta_{\upnu_{i}L}.

For
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, consider the shortest sequence of mutations

L\xrightarrow{i_{1}}\upnu_{i_{1}}L\to\ldots\xrightarrow{i_{n}}N,

where each step does not mutate the vertex
R
. As before, write
\Upphi_{L}
for the composition of the corresponding mutation functors, but now write
\upvarphi_{L}\colon\Uptheta_{L}\to\Uptheta
for the induced map on K-theory. Again, whenever
\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathds{k}
, we will abuse notation and also write
\upvarphi_{L}\colon\Uptheta_{L}\otimes\mathds{k}\to\Uptheta\otimes\mathds{k}
.

The following was established first in
[
W1
]
when
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial, using King stability. The
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial can now be dropped, following
[
IW2
]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

[
W1
,
IW2
]

Suppose that
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is a
3
-fold flopping contraction, such that
X
has only terminal singularities.
Then there is a finite decomposition

{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J})}=\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)}\upvarphi_{L}(C_{+})\subseteq\Uptheta\otimes\mathbb{R}

In particular, the following statements hold.

(1)

The open decomposition
\bigcup\upvarphi_{L}(C_{+})
gives the chambers of the
\mathcal{J}
-finite hyperplane arrangement.

(2)

If
L\ncong M
, then
\upvarphi_{L}(C_{+})
and
\upvarphi_{M}(C_{+})
do not intersect.

(3)

\upvarphi_{L}(C_{+})
and
\upvarphi_{M}(C_{+})
share a codimension one wall
\iff

L
and
M
differ by the mutation of an indecomposable summand.

For
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, write
C_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upvarphi_{L}(C_{+})
and set

(3.E)

\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Uptheta\otimes\mathbb{R})\backslash\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)}C_{L}.

By Theorem
3.6
(
1
),
\mathcal{H}
is a finite simplicial hyperplane arrangement, which by definition means that
\bigcap_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H=\{0\}
and all chambers in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}
are open simplicial cones.

3.4.
The Tracking Rules of Mutation

Given a modifying
R
-module
L
and any summand
L_{i}
,
\upnu_{i}\upnu_{i}L\cong L
by Proposition
2.1
(
2
). We will abuse notation and write

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}))
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}).
\scriptstyle\Upphi_{i}
\scriptstyle\Upphi_{i}

These, and their inverses, induce the following isomorphisms on K-theory

(3.F)

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to83.77pt{\vbox to31.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-32.46416pt\lower-15.93365pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Lemma 3.7
.

All four isomorphisms in (
3.F
) are given by the same matrix, and this matrix squares to the identity. If
i\neq 0
, then the same statement holds for
\upvarphi_{i},\upvarphi_{i}^{-1}
and
\Uptheta_{L},\Uptheta_{\upnu_{i}L}
.

Proof.

By (
3.B
), the matrices for the inverses are controlled by numbers appearing in the relevant approximation sequences. Suppose that the top
\upphi_{i}^{-1}
is controlled by numbers
b_{ij}
, and the bottom
\upphi_{i}^{-1}
is controlled by numbers
c_{ij}
. That the two matrices labelled
\upphi_{i}^{-1}
are the same is simply the statement that
b_{ij}=c_{ij}
, which is precisely the proof of
[
W1
, 5.22]
when
X
is
\mathds{Q}
-factorial, or
[
IW2
, 9.28]
generally. Given the fact that
b_{ij}=c_{ij}
, the statement that
\upphi_{i}^{-1}\upphi_{i}^{-1}={\rm Id}
can then simply be seen directly. Applying
\upphi_{i}
to each side then gives
\upphi_{i}^{-1}=\upphi_{i}
. All the statements on
\upvarphi_{i}
follow.
∎

3.5.
Complexified Actions

Via (
3.D
) and (
3.E
), associated to
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is an infinite real hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, and also a finite simplicial real hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
. Stability conditions will require the complexified versions of these.

By a slight abuse of notation, consider

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{x+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty\in(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\mid x_{j}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty_{j}\in\mathbb{H}\mbox{ for all }1\leq j\leq n\right\}\cong\mathbb{H}^{n}

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{x+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty\in(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\mid x_{j}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty_{j}\in\mathbb{H}\mbox{ for all }0\leq j\leq n\right\}\cong\mathbb{H}^{n+1}

where
\mathbb{H}=\{re^{\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\uppi\upvartheta}\in\mathbb{C}\mid r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,0<\upvartheta\leq 1\}\subset\mathbb{C}
is the semi-closed upper half plane. The regions
\mathbb{H}_{+}
and
\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\prime}
technically depend on
L
, since they are subsets of
(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
and
(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
respectively, but we drop this from the notation.

For
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, recall from §
3.3
that after choosing a mutation path
L\to\ldots\to N
that does not involve mutating
R
, there is a corresponding linear map
\upvarphi_{L}\colon(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}
.
We require the following result, where as usual
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the complexification of the real hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
. The result is folklore when the arrangement
\mathcal{H}
is Coxeter. Given our setting here is just mildly more general, and the proof is combinatorial in nature, we give a self-contained proof in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 3.8
.

There is an equality

(\Uptheta\otimes{\mathbb{C}})\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)}\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})

where the union on the right hand side is disjoint.

On the other hand, the affine version of Proposition
3.8
is a little bit more involved. We first pass to the
complexified level
, defined to be

(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\biggr\{z\in(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\Bigm|\sum_{j=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{j})z_{j}=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\biggr\},

and inside
(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
consider the region

\mathbb{E}_{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\biggr\{z\in\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\prime}\Bigm|\sum_{j=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{j})z_{j}=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\biggr\}.

.

z=x+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty
, then

\mathbb{H}_{+}&&\upvarphi_{1}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\end{array}

On the other hand, as in Example
3.5
, for any one-curve flop
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}
consists of infinitely many points on the real axis. To exhibit the region
\mathbb{E}_{+}
, note first that
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
if and only if we can write

(z_{0},z_{1})=((-\ell x_{1},1-\ell y_{1}),(x_{1},y_{1})),

where
\ell
is the length of the curve.
To belong to
\mathbb{E}_{+}
is equivalent to both factors being in
\mathbb{H}
. If the second factor is in
\mathbb{H}
then
y_{1}\geq 0
, so the first factor being in
\mathbb{H}
implies that
0\leq y_{1}\leq\frac{1}{\ell}
. In fact, it is elementary to check that
\mathbb{E}_{+}
forms the following region:

\scriptstyle 0
\scriptstyle\frac{1}{\ell}
\scriptstyle x_{1}
\scriptstyle y_{1}

The non-standard way of drawing the
x
and
y
axis is justified by Example
3.5
. The co-ordinate
y_{1}
should be viewed as the original
\operatorname{\sf Level}
seen in Example
3.5
, which naturally points to the left, and
x_{1}
should be viewed as the ‘complexified co-ordinate’. The other regions
\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+})
have the same shape as the above, sandwiched between the two adjacent dots, and so give a disjoint union that covers
(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Set
\mathcal{W}
to be the set of full hyperplanes in
\mathcal{K}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}
that separate the open chambers
\upphi_{L}(C_{+})
of
{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
(see e.g. Example
A.1
). We then consider the complexification of
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
in
\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
, defined to be

(3.G)

\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigcup_{W\in\mathcal{W}}(W_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}),

where
W_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}W\oplus\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0ptW
. As in Example
3.9
,
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}
can be viewed as the complexification of hyperplanes in the real level, provided that we swap the roles of
x
and
y
. Indeed, if we set
H_{W}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}W\cap\operatorname{\sf Level}
, then
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}=\bigcup_{W\in\mathcal{W}}H_{W}
, and the linear bijection
\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}\oplus\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\operatorname{\sf Level}
defined by
x+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty\mapsto(x+y)+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty
maps
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}
to
\bigcup_{W\in\mathcal{W}}(H_{W}\oplus\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0ptH_{W})
.

The following two results are evident, by inspection, for any one-curve flops using Example
3.9
above. The more general case requires a more involved combinatorial argument, so again the proofs are postponed until Appendix
A
.

Lemma 3.10

(
A.4
)
.

The subspace
\mathbb{E}_{+}\subset(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
is path connected.

Proposition 3.11
.

There is an equality

\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}=\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)}\upphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+}),

where the union on the right hand side is disjoint.

4.
Arrangement Groupoids

In this subsection we briefly recall the basics of the arrangement (=Deligne) groupoid of a locally finite real hyperplane arrangement, mainly to set notation. Some first results specific to the flops setting are presented in Subsection
4.2
.

4.1.
Arrangements groupoids

Throughout, let
\mathcal{H}
be either the finite real hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
from (
3.E
), or the infinite version
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
from (
3.D
). Both are
locally finite
arrangements, i.e. every point of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is contained in at most finitely many hyperplanes, and
essential arrangements
, i.e. the minimal intersections of hyperplanes are points.

Definition 4.1
.

The graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
of oriented arrows is defined as follows. The vertices of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
are the chambers (i.e. the connected components) of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}
. There is a unique arrow
a\colon v_{1}\to v_{2}
from chamber
v_{1}
to chamber
v_{2}
if the chambers are adjacent, otherwise there is no arrow. For an arrow
a\colon v_{1}\to v_{2}
, we set
s(a)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{1}
and
t(a)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{2}
. By definition, if there is an arrow
a\colon v_{1}\to v_{2}
, there is a unique arrow
b\colon v_{2}\to v_{1}
with the opposite direction of
a
.

A
positive path of length
n
in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
is defined to be a formal symbol

p=a_{n}\circ\ldots\circ a_{2}\circ a_{1},

whenever there exists a sequence of vertices
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}
of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
and exist arrows
a_{i}\colon v_{i-1}\to v_{i}
in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
. Set
s(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{0}
,
t(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{n}
, and
\ell(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}n
, and write
p\colon s(p)\to t(p)
. The notation
\circ
should remind us of composition, but we will often drop the
\circ
’s in future. If

q=b_{m}\circ\ldots\circ b_{2}\circ b_{1}
is another positive path with
t(p)=s(q)
, we consider the formal symbol

q\circ p\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b_{m}\circ\ldots\circ b_{2}\circ b_{1}\circ a_{n}\circ\ldots\circ a_{2}\circ a_{1},

and call it the
composition
of
p
and
q
.

Definition 4.2
.

A positive path is called
reduced
if it does not cross any hyperplane twice.

In our setting where
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, reduced positive paths coincide with shortest positive paths. In the finite setting this can be found in e.g.
[
P1
, 4.2]
, and in the infinite case see e.g.
[
S
, Lemma 2]
or
[
IM
, §I.5]
.

Following
[
D2
, p7]
, let
\sim
denote the smallest equivalence relation, compatible with morphism composition, that identifies all morphisms that arise as positive reduced paths with same source and target. Then consider the free category
\mathrm{Free}(\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}})
on the graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
, where morphisms are directed paths, and the quotient category

\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{Free}(\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}})/\sim,

called the category of positive paths.

Definition 4.3
.

The
arrangement (=Deligne) groupoid

\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the groupoid defined as the groupoid completion of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, that is, a formal inverse is added for every morphism in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
.

Notation 4.4
.

When
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}
from (
3.E
), we denote the arrangement groupoid by
\mathds{G}
, and when
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
from (
3.D
), we denote the arrangement groupoid by
\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

The following is well-known
[
D1
,
P1
,
P2
,
S
]
; in the level of generality here with
\mathcal{H}
locally finite and essential, the statement below is
[
D2
, p9]
.

Theorem 4.5
.

For any vertex
v
in the arrangement groupoid,
\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}}(v)\cong\uppi_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})
, and
\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(v)\cong\uppi_{1}(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

4.2.
First Results for Flops

This section proves that in our flops setting, pure braids act as the identity on K-theory. This will be crucial in showing that they act as deck transformations later in Section
6
. Throughout this subsection,
\mathcal{H}
is either
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, and
C_{L}
denotes the chamber in the complement of
\mathcal{H}
corresponding to either
\upvarphi_{L}(C_{+})
or
\operatorname{\sf Alcove}_{L}
, when
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
respectively. The following two lemmas are elementary.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
\upalpha\colon A\to B
is a reduced positive path for
\mathcal{H}
, and that
s_{i}
is a simple wall crossing separating
B
and
C
, so by definition there are morphisms
s_{i}\colon B\to C
and
s_{i}\colon C\to B
in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
. If
s_{i}\circ\upalpha\colon A\to C
is not reduced, then there exists some reduced positive path
\upgamma\colon A\to C
such that
s_{i}\circ\upgamma\colon A\to B
is reduced.

Proof.

This is very similar to
[
HW
, 5.1]
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
\upalpha=s_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{1}}
is a reduced positive path for
\mathcal{H}
, namely

\upalpha=(C_{1}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{1}}}C_{2}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{2}}}\ldots C_{t-1}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{t}}}C_{t})

where each
s_{i_{j}}
crosses a hyperplane
H_{j}
, say. Then for all
j=1,\ldots,t-1
, the chambers
C_{1}
and
C_{j}
are on the same side of
H_{j}
.

Proof.

There is nothing to prove in the case
j=1
. If
C_{1}
and
C_{j}
are on opposite sides of
H_{j}
for some
j>1
, then clearly
\upbeta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}s_{i_{j-1}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{1}}\colon C_{1}\to C_{j}
must at some point cross
H_{j}
. But then
s_{i_{j}}\circ\upbeta
, and hence
\upalpha
, must cross
H_{j}
twice, which is a contradiction.
∎

With respect to our applications, part (2) of the following proposition is crucial. For any positive
\upalpha\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
, say
\upalpha={s_{i_{t}}}\circ\ldots\circ{s_{i_{1}}}
we associate the functor

\Upphi_{\upalpha}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Upphi_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{1}}.

Now there is an order
\geq
defined on tilting modules (see e.g.
[
HW
, §3]
). By Lemma
4.7
, this order decreases along reduced paths, and so exactly as in
[
HW
, 4.6]
(see Remark
4.10
below), we see that
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\Upphi_{\upbeta}
for any two reduced positive paths with the same start and end points. Hence the association
\upalpha\mapsto\Upphi_{\upalpha}
descends to a functor
from
(\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})^{+}
. As
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
is already invertible, this in turn formally descends to a functor from
\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

The same analysis holds for the finite situation
\mathds{G}
. In both cases, for any
\upalpha
in the arrangement groupoid, we thus have an associated functor
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
, and its image
\upphi_{\upalpha}
on K-theory
\mathcal{K}
, respectively
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}
on
\Uptheta
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Choose a reduced positive path
\upbeta\colon C_{L}\to C_{M}
for
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

(1)

If
\upalpha\colon C_{L}\to C_{M}
is any positive path in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
, then
\upphi_{\upalpha}=\upphi_{\upbeta}
.

(2)

If
p\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{L})
, then
\upphi_{p}=\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{K}_{L}}
.

(3)

If
p,q\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{L},C_{M})
, then
\upphi_{p}=\upphi_{q}
.

The same statements hold for
\mathcal{H}
, replacing
\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
by
\mathds{G}
, and
\upphi
by
\upvarphi
.

Proof.

We will establish all statements over
\mathbb{Z}
, as then all the statements over
\mathds{k}
follow.

(1) By the discussion above the Proposition, we know that if
\upalpha
is furthermore reduced, then
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\Upphi_{\upbeta}
, and so in particular
\upphi_{\upalpha}=\upphi_{\upbeta}
holds. Hence we can assume that
\upalpha
is not reduced.

Consider the first time that
\upalpha={s_{i_{t}}}\circ\ldots\circ{s_{i_{1}}}
crosses a hyperplane twice. So, say
\upbeta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}s_{i_{m-1}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{1}}
is reduced, but
s_{i_{m}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{1}}
is not. Pictorially

C_{L}=C_{1}
C_{m-1}
C_{m}
H
\scriptstyle\upbeta
\scriptstyle s_{i_{m}}

By Lemma
4.6
we can find a positive reduced path
\upgamma\colon C_{1}\to C_{m}
such that the composition
C_{1}\xrightarrow{\upgamma}C_{m}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{m}}}C_{m-1}
is reduced. As
\upbeta
and
s_{i_{m}}\circ\upgamma
are reduced paths with the same start and end points, functorially they are the same, so

\displaystyle\Upphi_{\upalpha}

\displaystyle=\Upphi_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{m}}\circ(\Upphi_{i_{m-1}}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{1}})

\displaystyle\cong\Upphi_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{m}}\circ(\Upphi_{i_{m}}\circ\Upphi_{\upgamma})

Passing to K-theory, using the fact that
\upphi_{i_{m}}\upphi_{i_{m}}=\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
by Lemma
3.7
, we see

\upphi_{\upalpha}=\upphi_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ\upphi_{i_{m+1}}\circ\upphi_{\upgamma}.

Consider next the first time that
s_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{m+1}}\circ\upgamma
crosses a hyperplane twice. Since
\upgamma
is reduced, we move further to the left. Applying the above argument repeatedly, by induction we end up in the case of a reduced path, and hence
\upphi_{\upalpha}=\upphi_{\upbeta}
.

(2) Say
\upphi_{p}=\upphi_{i_{n}}^{\pm 1}\circ\ldots\circ\upphi_{i_{1}}^{\pm 1}
for some choice of superscripts
\pm 1
. By Lemma
3.7
,
\upphi_{p}=\upphi_{q}
, where
q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}s_{i_{n}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{1}}
. This is a positive path, with start and end
C_{L}
, so by part (1) it follows that
\upphi_{p}=\upphi_{q}=\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.

(3) This follows by applying (2) to
q^{-1}p\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}_{\mathcal{H}}}(C_{L})
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

Proposition
4.8
also implies that Theorems
3.3
and
3.6
, and also Propositions
3.8
and
3.11
, can be indexed over reduced positive paths terminating at
C_{+}
.

Remark 4.10
.

Implicit in the above analysis is the fact, proved in
[
HW
, 4.6]
in the finite case and
[
IW2
, 9.34]
in the infinite case, that if
\upalpha\colon L\to M
is a positive minimal path, then
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\Lambda_{L}}(\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,M),-)
. Hence any two positive minimal paths give rise to isomorphic functors.

5.
Stability Conditions, Tilting and t-structure Transfer

5.1.
Generalities on stability conditions

We will not give a full summary of stability conditions here; see for example
[
B4
]
or the survey
[
B1
]
. For our purposes, the following suffices.
Throughout this subsection,
\mathcal{T}
denotes a triangulated category for which the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathcal{T})
is a finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

Proposition 5.1

(
[
B4
, 5.3]
)
.

To specify a stability condition on
\mathcal{T}
is equivalent to specifying a bounded t-structure on
\mathcal{T}
with heart
\mathcal{A}
, together with a stability function
Z
on
\mathcal{A}
that satisfies the Harder-Narasimhan property.

As usual, in fact we will only study
locally finite
stability conditions, and we let
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}
denote the set of locally finite stability conditions on
\mathcal{T}
. There is a topology on
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}
, induced by a natural metric.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
B4
, 1.2]
)
.

The space
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}
has the structure of a complex manifold, and the forgetful map

\uppi\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{T}),\mathbb{C})

is a local isomorphism onto an open subspace of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{T}),\mathbb{C})
.

Remark 5.3
.

In the components
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
and
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
we study in the flops setting below, all stability conditions will automatically be
full
, in the sense that they are always modelled on the whole of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{T}),\mathbb{C})
. In particular, our stability conditions will automatically satisfy the
support property
, see e.g.
[
BM
, Appendix B]
. We will freely use this throughout.

An exact equivalence of triangulated categories
\Phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
induces a natural map

\Phi_{*}\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}\to\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}^{\prime}

defined by
\Phi_{*}(Z,\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(Z\circ\upphi^{-1},\Phi(\mathcal{A}))
, where as before
\upphi^{-1}
denotes the isomorphism on K-theory
K_{0}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{0}(\mathcal{T})
induced by the functor
\Phi^{-1}
, and
\Phi(\mathcal{A})
denotes its essential image. As usual, if two exact equivalences
\Phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and
\Psi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are naturally isomorphic, then
\Phi_{*}(Z,\mathcal{A})=\Psi_{*}(Z,\mathcal{A})
for any
(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}
, and thus the group
\operatorname{Auteq}(\mathcal{T})
of isomorphism classes of autoequivalences of
\mathcal{T}
acts on
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{T}
.

5.2.
Stability, Normalisation and Mutations

We return to the setting where
f\colon X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is the flopping contraction as in the introduction, with distinguished
R
-module
N
from (
2.C
),
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{R}(N)
, and K-theory
\mathcal{K}
and
\Uptheta
from Sections
3.2
and
3.3
respectively.

For any
L=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, with the ordering on summands induced from
N
as explained under Definition
2.2
, let
\mathcal{S}_{i}
be the simple
\Lambda_{L}
-module corresponding to the projective
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\operatorname{Hom}(L,L_{i})
. Write
\mathcal{B}_{L}
for the subcategory of
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
consisting of finite-length modules. If
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, then we further write
\mathcal{A}_{L}
for the full subcategory of
\mathcal{B}_{L}
of those finite-length modules whose simple factors are not isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.

Consider the triangulated subcategories

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{L}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{a\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\mid\mathrm{H}^{i}(a)\in\mathcal{A}_{L}\mbox{ for all $i$}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{L}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{b\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\mid\mathrm{H}^{i}(b)\in\mathcal{B}_{L}\mbox{ for all $i$}\}.

Since
\mathcal{A}_{L}
and
\mathcal{B}_{L}
are extension closed abelian subcategories of
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
, the standard t-structure on
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})
restricts to a bounded t-structure on
\mathcal{D}_{L}
with heart
\mathcal{B}_{L}
, and a bounded t-structure on
\mathcal{C}_{L}
with heart
\mathcal{A}_{L}
.

The categories
\mathcal{A}_{L}
and
\mathcal{B}_{L}
have finitely many simple objects, and so

K_{0}(\mathcal{C}_{L})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{S}_{i}]\qquad K_{0}(\mathcal{D}_{L})\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{S}_{i}].

There are canonical isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{C}_{L}),\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\qquad\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(K_{0}(\mathcal{D}_{L}),\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}

given by
\upgamma\mapsto\sum\upgamma([\mathcal{S}_{i}])[\mathcal{P}_{i}]
. Composing these with the local homeomorphism in Theorem
5.2
defines local homeomorphisms

\mathcal{Z}_{L}\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{C}_{L}\to(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\qquad\mathcal{Z}_{L}\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{D}_{L}\to(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}.

Write
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{L}
for the stability functions on
\mathcal{A}_{L}
which satisfy the Harder–Narasimhan property, then by Proposition
5.1

\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{L}
can be regarded as a subspace of
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{C}_{L}
. Similarly for
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{L}
, which is a subspace of
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{D}_{L}
. It follows from
[
B4
, 5.2]
that the above local homeomorphisms restrict to isomorphisms

(5.A)

\mathcal{Z}_{L}\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{H}_{+}\qquad\mathcal{Z}_{L}\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{+}.

Applying all of the above to
N\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, it will be convenient to suppress
N
from the notation, so write
\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{D}_{N}
,
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{B}_{N}
,
\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{Z}_{N}
, etc.

There is a
\mathbb{C}
-action on
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{D}
, which later we will avoid. As such, following
[
B6
]
, for any
L=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, consider
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}_{L}
to be those stability conditions in
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{D}_{L}
for which the central charge
Z
satisfies

\sum_{j=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{j})\,Z[\mathcal{S}_{j}]=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt.

We call such stability conditions
normalised
.
In particular

\mathcal{Z}_{L}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}_{L}\to(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}},

where the complexified level is defined in §
3.5
. Set
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{L}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}_{L}
, then the latter isomorphism in (
5.A
) restricts to an isomorphism

\mathcal{Z}_{L}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}_{L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{E}_{+}.

Proposition 5.4
.

L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
