\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.533pt}{-22.50328pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 118.11 -10.73 L 118.11 -47.77}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{85.35828pt}{-34.72392pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{88.89128pt}{-23.73123pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

Proof.

To ease notation, set
\mathcal{Q}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,L_{i})
and write
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}
for the simple
\Lambda_{L}
-module corresponding to the projective
\Lambda_{L}
-module
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. Similarly, write
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,N_{i})
, and
\mathcal{S}_{i}
for the corresponding simple
\Lambda
-module.

Consider the perfect pairing

\upchi(-,-)\colon\Uptheta\times K_{0}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{Z}

given by
\upchi(a,b)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{i}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}(a,b[i])
.
Since the pairing is perfect, setting

a_{ij}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upchi(\Upphi_{L}(\mathcal{Q}_{i}),\mathcal{S}_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}

implies that
[\Upphi_{L}(\mathcal{Q}_{i})]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}[\mathcal{P}_{j}]
in
\Uptheta
.
Furthermore, since
\Upphi_{L}^{-1}
is right adjoint to
\Upphi_{L}
,

a_{ij}=\upchi(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Upphi_{L}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{j})),

which in turn implies that
[\Upphi_{L}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{j})]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{ij}[\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}]
in
K_{0}(\mathcal{C})
.
Therefore, for any point
\upsigma=(Z,\mathcal{P})\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{L}
, necessarily

\upvarphi_{L}(\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z(\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime})[\Upphi_{L}(\mathcal{Q}_{i})]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{ij}Z(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i})\Big)[\mathcal{P}_{j}]=\mathcal{Z}({\Upphi_{L}}_{*}(\upsigma)).

The last diagram follows immediately, as mutation functors in
K
-theory take
\sum(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{i})[\mathcal{S}_{i}]
to
\sum(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{i})[\mathcal{S}_{i}]
and thus preserve the normalisation.
∎

5.3.
Tilting at Simples via Mutation

By the above,
\mathcal{A}_{L}\subset\mathcal{C}_{L}
and
\mathcal{B}_{L}\subset\mathcal{D}_{L}
are the hearts of bounded t-structures, with finitely many simples. Each of these simple objects induces two torsion theories,
(\langle\mathcal{S}_{i}\rangle,\mathcal{F}_{i})
and
(\mathcal{T}_{i},\langle\mathcal{S}_{i}\rangle)
, where
\langle\mathcal{S}_{i}\rangle
is the full subcategory of objects whose simple factors are isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. In the case of
\mathcal{A}_{L}
, the subcategories
\mathcal{F}_{i}
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{L}\mid\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}_{L}}(\mathcal{S}_{i},a)=0\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{i}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{L}\mid\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}_{L}}(a,\mathcal{S}_{i})=0\},

and the corresponding tilted hearts are defined by

\displaystyle{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{L})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{c\in\mathcal{C}_{L}\mid{\rm H}^{k}(c)=0\mbox{ for }k\notin\{0,1\},\,{\rm H}^{0}(c)\in\mathcal{F}_{i},\,{\rm H}^{1}(c)\in\langle\mathcal{S}_{i}\rangle\}

\displaystyle{\rm R}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{L})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{c\in\mathcal{C}_{L}\mid{\rm H}^{k}(c)=0\mbox{ for }k\notin\{-1,0\},\,{\rm H}^{-1}(c)\in\langle\mathcal{S}_{i}\rangle,\,{\rm H}^{0}(c)\in\mathcal{T}_{i}\},

where
{\rm H}^{i}(-)
is the cohomological functor associated to the standard t-structure on
\mathcal{C}_{L}
defining
\mathcal{A}_{L}
. A similar picture applies in the case of
\mathcal{B}_{L}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have
{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})=\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{A})
and
{\rm R}_{i}(\mathcal{A})=\Upphi_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. The same statements hold replacing
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathcal{B}
, for all
i=0,1,\ldots,n

Proof.

We will only show that
{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})=\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{A})
, since the other proof is similar. Since both categories are hearts of bounded t-structures, it suffices to show that
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})
. For this, since
\mathcal{A}
is finite length, it is enough to show that
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j})\in{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})
for all
1\leq j\leq n
.

If
j=i
, since
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{i})=\mathcal{S}_{i}[-1]
by
[
W1
, 4.15(2)]
, it follows that
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{i})\in{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})
. Hence we can assume that
j\neq i
. Then
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N}
by
[
HW
, 4.4]
. Hence
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j})\cong{\rm H}^{0}(\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j}))
, and so
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j})
is only in degree zero, and further

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}({\upnu_{i}\Lambda})}(\mathcal{S}_{i},{\rm H}^{0}(\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j})))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\Lambda)}(\mathcal{S}_{i},\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j}))

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\Lambda)}(\Upphi_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{i}),\mathcal{S}_{j})

(by
[
HW
, (4.B)]
)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\Lambda)}(\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{\upnu_{i}\Lambda}(\mathcal{S}_{i},T_{i})[1],\mathcal{S}_{j})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Ext}^{-1}_{\Lambda}(\operatorname{Tor}_{1}^{\upnu_{i}\Lambda}(\mathcal{S}_{i},T_{i}),\mathcal{S}_{j})=0.

Combining, it follows that
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{j})\in{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\upnu_{i}N})
.
∎

5.4.
t-structure transfer

In moving to the mutation functors, which reveals many hidden t-structures, we lose control over Fourier–Mukai techniques. The following theorem is one of our main results, and is a crucial ingredient in the proof of Theorem
6.9
later.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
,
\upalpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{L},C_{L})
, and consider

\Upphi_{\upalpha}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}).

Then the following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

\Upphi_{\upalpha}
maps the simples
\mathcal{S}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{n}
to simples.

(2)

\Upphi_{\upalpha}\colon\mathcal{D}_{L}\to\mathcal{D}_{L}
restricts to an equivalence
\mathcal{B}_{L}\to\mathcal{B}_{L}
.

(3)

\Upphi_{\upalpha}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})
restricts to an equivalence
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}\to\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
.

(4)

There is a functorial isomorphism
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.

If further
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
and
\upalpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}}(C_{L},C_{L})
, the above are equivalent to

(5)

\Upphi_{\upalpha}
maps the simples
\mathcal{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{n}
to simples.

(6)

\Upphi_{\upalpha}
restricts to an equivalence
\mathcal{A}_{L}\to\mathcal{A}_{L}
.

When the last additional conditions are satisfied, it is already known that (5)
\Rightarrow
(2) by
[
HW
, 5.5]
. Furthermore, it is clear that (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2), (5)
\Leftrightarrow
(6) and (4)
\Rightarrow
(1)(5). Hence to prove Theorem
5.6
it suffices to show that (1)
\Rightarrow
(3) and (3)
\Rightarrow
(4).

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\Gamma
be a noetherian ring, and
x\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)
. Then the following hold.

(1)

x\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)^{\leq 0}\iff\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\Gamma}(x,S)=0
for all
i<0
and all simple
\Gamma
-modules
S
.

(2)

If a triangulated equivalence
F\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)
satisfies
F(\Gamma)\cong\Gamma
, then
F
restricts to an equivalence
F\colon\operatorname{mod}\Gamma\to\operatorname{mod}\Gamma
.

Proof.

(1) The direction (
\Rightarrow
) is clear, by replacing
x
by its projective resolution
P
, and observing that

\operatorname{Ext}_{\Gamma}^{i}(x,S)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{K}^{-}\!(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)}(P,S[i])=0
for all
i<0
, since there are no chain maps between the complexes
P
and
S[i]
.

For (
\Leftarrow
), since
x
is bounded, let
t
be maximum such that
{\rm H}^{t}(x)\neq 0
. Since
\Gamma
is noetherian, every finitely generated module has a map to a simple, so there exists some simple
S
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}({\rm H}^{t}(x),S)\neq 0
. But via the spectral sequence (see e.g.
[
H2
, (2.8)]
)

E_{2}^{p,q}=\operatorname{Ext}^{p}_{\Gamma}({\rm H}^{-q}(x),S)\Rightarrow\operatorname{Ext}_{\Gamma}^{p+q}(x,S)

the nonzero
E_{2}^{0,-t}
term survives to give a non-zero element of
\operatorname{Ext}^{-t}_{\Gamma}(x,S)
. Hence
t\leq 0
.

(2) Via the isomorphism
{\rm H}^{n}(x)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)}(\Gamma,x[n])
, it follows that
F
and its inverse take modules to modules, and so they restrict to a Morita equivalence.
∎

The following establishes (1)
\Rightarrow
(3). In fact, we prove a slightly more general version, as we will need this later.

Corollary 5.8
.

Suppose that
G\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})
is any equivalence that maps the simples
\mathcal{S}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{n}
to simples. Then
G
restricts to an equivalence
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}\to\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
.

Proof.

To ease notation, set
\mathcal{E}=\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})
. By Lemma
5.7
(
1
) it follows that both
G
and its inverse restrict to an equivalence
\mathcal{E}^{\leq 0}\to\mathcal{E}^{\leq 0}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}^{\geq 1}
can be characterised as the perpendicular to
\mathcal{E}^{\leq 0}
, it follows that both
G
and its inverse restrict to an equivalence
\mathcal{E}^{\geq 1}\to\mathcal{E}^{\geq 1}
. Since
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}=\mathcal{E}^{\leq 0}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\geq 1}[1]
, the result follows.
∎

The implication (3)
\Rightarrow
(4) is by far the most subtle. It requires the following two technical results, both of which rely heavily on the fact that
R
is isolated cDV.

Proposition 5.9
.

In the setting of Theorem
5.6
, if
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
restricts to
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
, then
\Upphi_{i}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i}
restricts to
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
.

Proof.

Consider the functor
G=\Upphi_{i}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i}
. For each
j\neq i
, as in Lemma
3.2
we have
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{P}_{j})\cong\mathcal{P}_{j}
. Since
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
restricts to an equivalence on
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
, and is necessarily the identity on
\mathcal{K}_{L}
by Proposition
4.8
, furthermore
\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\mathcal{P}_{j})\cong\mathcal{P}_{j}
. In conclusion, whenever
j\neq i
we have
G(\mathcal{P}_{j})\cong\mathcal{P}_{j}
. In a similar vein, by
[
W1
, 4.15(2)]
,
\Upphi_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{i})\cong\mathcal{S}_{i}[-1]
. The functor
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
must send simples to simples, and since it is the identity on
\mathcal{K}_{L}
, by the pairing between projectives and simples it follows that
\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\mathcal{S}_{i})\cong\mathcal{S}_{i}
. Thus
G(\mathcal{S}_{i})\cong\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

By the assumptions, since
\Lambda_{L}
is basic, necessarily
\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\Lambda_{L})\cong\Lambda_{L}
. Now consider
\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\upnu_{i}L,L)
defining the functor
\Upphi_{i}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L})
. By construction
G(\mathcal{T})\cong\Upphi_{i}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\Lambda_{L})\cong\Upphi_{i}^{-1}(\Lambda_{L})\cong\mathcal{T}
. Since
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\mathcal{P}_{j}
, by the above paragraph necessarily
G(\mathcal{T}_{i})\cong\mathcal{T}_{i}
.

Now by e.g.
[
W1
, A.2(2)]
the exchange sequences give rise to an exact sequence of
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
-modules

0\to\mathcal{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{b}\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\oplus a_{ij}}\to\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\oplus a_{ij}}\to\mathcal{P}_{i}\to\frac{\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}}{(1-e_{i})}\to 0

with
\operatorname{Cok}b\cong\mathcal{T}_{i}
, where
e_{i}
is the idempotent corresponding to the
i
th summand of
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
, and
(1-e_{i})
is the two-sided ideal generated by
1-e_{i}
. Since
R
is isolated, necessarily
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}/(1-e_{i})
has finite length, and is filtered only by the simple
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. Splicing gives triangles

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i}\to\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\oplus a_{ij}}\to\mathcal{T}_{i}\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{i}\to\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\oplus a_{ij}}\to K_{i}\to

\displaystyle K_{i}\to\mathcal{P}_{i}\to\frac{\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}}{(1-e_{i})}\to

Applying
G
to each, the first triangle shows that
\mathrm{H}^{t}(G(\mathcal{P}_{i}))=0
unless
t=0,1
. On the other hand, the second triangle shows that
\mathrm{H}^{t}(G(K_{i}))=0
unless
t=-1,0
. But
G
must take
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}/(1-e_{i})
to degree zero, since
G(\mathcal{S}_{i})\cong\mathcal{S}_{i}
and
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}/(1-e_{i})
is filtered by
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. Hence the last triangle implies that
\mathrm{H}^{t}(G(\mathcal{P}_{i}))=0
unless
t=-1,0
.

Combining, we see that
\mathrm{H}^{t}(G(\mathcal{P}_{i}))=0
unless
t=0
, thus
G(\mathcal{P}_{i})
is a module. Applying
G
to the first triangle give a triangle

G(\mathcal{P}_{i})\to\bigoplus_{j\neq i}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\oplus a_{ij}}\to\mathcal{T}_{i}\to

in which all terms are modules, so this is necessarily induced by a short exact sequence. Hence by the depth lemma,
G(\mathcal{P}_{i})
has depth
3
. On the other hand, since
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is perfect as a complex, so is
G(\mathcal{P}_{i})
, thus
G(\mathcal{P}_{i})
has finite projective dimension as a
\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
-module. By Auslander–Buchsbaum
[
IW1
, 2.16]
, it follows that
G(\mathcal{P}_{i})
is projective. Since
G
is the identity on
\mathcal{K}_{\upnu_{i}L}
, necessarily
G(\mathcal{P}_{i})\cong\mathcal{P}_{i}
and hence
G(\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L})\cong\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
. By Lemma
5.7
(
2
),
G=\Upphi_{i}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i}
restricts to an equivalence
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}\to\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upnu_{i}L}
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Suppose that
G\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
is an
R
-linear equivalence such that
G(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cong\mathcal{S}_{0}
, and suppose that there exists a permutation
\upiota\in S_{n}
such that
G(\mathcal{S}_{i})\cong\mathcal{S}_{\upiota(i)}
and
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{i}\cong\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{\upiota(i)}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. Then functorially
G\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.

Proof.

By Corollary
5.8
,
G
maps projectives to projectives. Since
G(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cong\mathcal{S}_{0}
, by the pairing between simples and projectives, necessarily
G(\mathcal{P}_{0})\cong\mathcal{P}_{0}
. Consider the
R
-linear composition
F
given by

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
F
G
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X},-)
-\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}_{X}

By
[
K1
, 5.2.4]
the functor
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X},-)
maps skyscrapers of closed points to modules of dimension vector
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{N}=(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
which satisfy the
\star
-generated condition, which by definition consists of those
\Lambda
-modules
A
of dimension vector
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{N}
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(A,\mathcal{S}_{i})=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,n

[
SY
, 6.11]
. For any such
A
, by the assumptions on where
G
takes each simple,
GA
has dimension vector
(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{\upiota(i)})_{i=0}^{n}
, which by the last assumption is precisely
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{N}
. Furthermore, for any such
A
, since
G
fixes
\mathcal{S}_{0}
and permutes the other simples,
GA
is also
\star
-generated. Hence again appealing to
[
K1
, 5.2.4]
the functor
-\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}_{X}
takes the module
GA
to a skyscraper. Combining, we see that skyscrapers of closed points get sent to skyscrapers of closed points, under the above
R
-linear composition
F
.

It follows from general Fourier–Mukai theory
[
BM2
, §3.3]
that
F\cong\varphi_{*}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
where
\varphi\colon X\to X
is an automorphism and
\mathcal{L}
is some line bundle. Since
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V}_{X},-)
sends
\mathcal{O}_{X}
to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
, and
G
sends
\mathcal{P}_{0}
to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
, it follows that
F
sends
\mathcal{O}_{X}
to
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, which in turn implies that
\mathcal{L}
is trivial. Lastly, since
F\cong\varphi_{*}
is
R
-linear, by Proposition
2.4

\varphi
commutes with the map to the base. In particular, the restriction of
\varphi
to the dense open subset
U=X\backslash C
is the identity. Hence
\varphi=\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits_{X}
, and as a result,
F\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
. From this, it follows that
G\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.
∎

Finally, we prove (3)
\Rightarrow
(4), completing the proof of Theorem
5.6
. The key is that Proposition
5.9
allows us to pull everything back to
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
, where we can use the geometric Fourier–Mukai techniques of Proposition
5.10
.

Corollary 5.11
.

In the setting of Theorem
5.6
, if
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
restricts to
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{L}
, then there is a functorial isomorphism
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.

Proof.

Choose a positive path
\upgamma\colon C_{+}\to C_{L}
, and consider the composition

G=\Upphi_{\upgamma}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{\upgamma}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)

Since
\upgamma
is a composition
s_{i_{t}}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i_{1}}
, we may rewrite the above as

G=\Upphi_{i_{1}}^{-1}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{t-1}}^{-1}\circ(\Upphi_{i_{t}}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i_{t}})\circ\Upphi_{i_{t-1}}\circ\ldots\circ\Upphi_{i_{1}}

By induction, using Proposition
5.9
repeatedly, we see that
G
restricts to an equivalence on
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda
. Since Morita equivalences preserve projectives, and
G
is the identity on K-theory
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{N}
by Proposition
4.8
,
G
maps each projective to itself. Since Morita equivalences also preserve simples, by the pairing between projectives and simples,
G
maps each simple to itself. By Proposition
5.10

G\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
and hence

\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.
∎

6.
Stability Conditions on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}

Consider
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
, the connected component of
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{C}
containing
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}
, and similarly
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
, the connected component of
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
containing
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}
. In this section we describe both
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
as regular covers of the hyperplane arrangements in Section
3
.

6.1.
Chamber Decomposition

For the fixed
R
-module
N
from (
2.C
), consider the set of morphisms in
\mathds{G}
which terminate at
C_{+}
, namely

\displaystyle\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}}(C_{L},C_{+}).

The set
\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})
is defined similarly, taking the union instead over
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
and replacing
\mathds{G}
by
\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

Notation 6.1
.

For
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, respectively
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, consider the open subsets

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{L}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(Z,\mathcal{A}_{L})\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{L}\mid\operatorname{Im}(Z[\mathcal{S}_{i}])>0\mbox{ \rm for all }i=1,\ldots,n\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{L}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(Z,\mathcal{B}_{L})\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{L}\mid\operatorname{Im}(Z[\mathcal{S}_{i}])>0\mbox{ \rm for all }i=0,1,\ldots,n\}

\displaystyle\mathds{N}_{L}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{U}_{L}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}_{L}=\mathbb{U}_{L}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}_{L}

of
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}_{L}
,
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}_{L}
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}_{L}
respectively. For
\upalpha\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
,
\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})
, set

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{2pt}}c@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{25pt}}r@{\hspace{2pt}}c@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}l@{\hspace{2pt}}}\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{\upalpha}\hskip 2.0&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&(\Upphi_{\upalpha})_{*}(\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{s(\upalpha)})\hfil\hskip 25.0&\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{\upbeta}\hskip 2.0&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{s(\upbeta)})\hfil\hskip 2.0\\
\mathrm{U}_{\upalpha}\hskip 2.0&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&(\Upphi_{\upalpha})_{*}(\mathrm{U}_{s(\upalpha)})\hfil\hskip 25.0&\mathbb{U}_{\upbeta}\hskip 2.0&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hfil\hskip 2.0&(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\mathbb{U}_{s(\upbeta)}),\hfil\hskip 2.0\end{array}

where
s(\upalpha)
and
s(\upbeta)
denote the modules corresponding to the chambers which are the sources of
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
respectively. Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}_{\upbeta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}_{s(\upbeta)})

\displaystyle\mathds{N}_{\upbeta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\mathds{N}_{s(\upbeta)}).

As usual, write
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{U}_{N}
,
\mathbb{U}=\mathbb{U}_{N}
and
\mathds{N}=\mathds{N}_{N}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Given
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
, respectively
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})
, write
M_{s(\upalpha)}
and
M_{s(\upbeta)}
for the modules corresponding to the chambers which are the sources of
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
respectively. Then

(1)

\mathrm{U}_{\upalpha}\cap\mathrm{U}_{\upbeta}\neq\emptyset

\iff

M_{s(\upalpha)}\cong M_{s(\upbeta)}
and
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\Upphi_{\upbeta}
.

(2)

\mathbb{U}_{\upalpha}\cap\mathbb{U}_{\upbeta}\neq\emptyset

\iff

M_{s(\upalpha)}\cong M_{s(\upbeta)}
and
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\Upphi_{\upbeta}

\iff

\mathds{N}_{\upalpha}\cap\mathds{N}_{\upbeta}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

(1) It suffices to show that, for
\upgamma\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
,
\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
M_{s(\upgamma)}\cong N
and
\Upphi_{\upgamma}\cong{\rm id}_{\mathcal{C}}
. The implication
(\Leftarrow)
is obvious, since the isomorphisms
M_{s(\upgamma)}\cong N
and
\Upphi_{\upgamma}\cong{\rm id}_{\mathcal{C}}
implies that
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}\neq\emptyset
, and write
\mathcal{Y}\colon\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{R}}
for the composition

\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Im}}\Uptheta_{\mathbb{R}}

where the last map is the projection defined by taking the imaginary parts. By definition
\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{U})=C_{+}
and
\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma})=\upvarphi_{M_{s(\upgamma)}}(C_{+})
.
Since
\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}\neq\emptyset
, necessarily
\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{U})\cap\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma})\neq\emptyset
, thus
M_{s(\upgamma)}\cong N
by Theorem
3.6
, and
\Upphi_{\upgamma}
is an autoequivalence of
\mathcal{C}
.
It is clear that
\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}\neq\emptyset
implies that
\Upphi_{\upgamma}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
maps
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{A}
. By Theorem
5.6
, this implies that

\Upphi_{\upgamma}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
.

(2) The proof of the first
\iff
is identical, appealing to Theorem
3.3
instead of Theorem
3.6
to deduce that
\Upphi_{\upgamma}
maps
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{B}
. Theorem
5.6
again implies that

\Upphi_{\upgamma}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
. The second
\iff
follows immediately from the first.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

For
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
with
l(\upalpha)>l(\upbeta)
, the chambers
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{\upalpha}
and
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{\upbeta}
share a codimension one boundary if and only if there exists a length one path
\upgamma\in{\rm Mor}(\mathds{G}^{+})
such that
\upalpha=\upbeta\circ\upgamma
or
\upalpha=\upbeta\circ\upgamma^{-1}
in
{\rm Mor}(\mathds{G})
. A similar statement holds replacing
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
by
\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})
and
\mathds{G}
by
\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
respectively.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that, for
\upgamma\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
,
\mathrm{U}
and
\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}
share a codimension one boundary if and only if there is a length one path
\updelta
such that
\upgamma=\updelta
or
\upgamma=\updelta^{-1}
. This follows from
[
B5
, 5.5]
and Lemma
5.5
.
∎

The following is the analogue of
[
T
, 4.11]
.

Theorem 6.4
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

There is a disjoint union of open chambers

\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\kern-12.0pt\bigcup_{\upalpha\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})}\kern-12.0pt\mathrm{U}_{\upalpha}\quad\subset\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}.

Furthermore,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}=\bigcup\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{\upalpha}=\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
, where
\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{\upalpha}
is the closure of
\mathrm{U}_{\upalpha}
in
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

There is a disjoint union of open chambers

\mathcal{N}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\kern-10.0pt\bigcup_{\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})}\kern-10.0pt{\mathbb{U}}_{\upbeta}\quad\subset\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}.

Furthermore,
\overline{\mathcal{N}}=\bigcup\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{\upbeta}=\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
.

In particular, as
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D},
there is a disjoint union of open chambers

\mathcal{N}_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\kern-10.0pt\bigcup_{\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})}\kern-10.0pt{\mathds{N}}_{\upbeta}\quad\subset\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}

such that
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{n}}=\bigcup\overline{{\mathds{N}}}_{\upbeta}=\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

(1) By Lemma
6.2
,
\mathcal{M}
is a disjoint union, and by Lemma
6.3

\overline{\mathcal{M}}
is connected. Since
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
contains
\overline{\mathrm{U}}
and thus
\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}
,
there is an inclusion
\overline{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
.

Let
\upsigma\in\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
be a point, and choose a point
\upsigma_{0}\in\mathrm{U}
and a path

p\colon[0,1]\to\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}

such that
p(0)=\upsigma_{0}
and
p(1)=\upsigma
. Since
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}
is a local homeomorphism, by deforming
p
if necessary, we may assume that the path
\mathcal{Z}\circ p\colon[0,1]\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}
passes through only finitely many codimension one boundaries of chambers
\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})
. Thus there exists a sequence
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{\ell-1}<t_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}1
of real numbers such that:

(a)

for all
i\neq\ell
, every
\mathcal{Z}(p(t_{i}))
is in a codimension one boundary of some chamber,

(b)

for all
i
, each open interval
\mathcal{Z}(p(t_{i},t_{i+1}))
is contained in the interior of some chamber.

Since
p((0,t_{1}))\subset\mathrm{U}
, by Lemma
6.3
there is a length one path
\upgamma\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits_{0}(C_{+})
such that
p(t_{1},t_{2})
is in
\mathrm{U}_{\upgamma}
. By iterating this argument, we see that
p((t_{l-1},1))
is in some open chamber
\mathrm{U}_{\upalpha}
, and hence its end point,
\upsigma
, belongs to
\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{\upalpha}
.

(2) This follows using an identical argument to (1).

For the last statements, we first prove that
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
. Let
\upsigma\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
. Since
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
is a connected and locally Euclidian space, it is path connected. Hence there is a path from
\upsigma
to a point
\upsigma_{0}\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
. Thus
\upsigma
also lies in
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
, proving
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
.

For the opposite inclusion, it is enough to show that
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
is connected, since
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
contains
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}
. But by (
2
),

\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}=(\bigcup\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}_{\upbeta})\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}=\bigcup\overline{{\mathds{N}}}_{\upbeta}.

Since
\overline{{\mathds{N}}}_{\upbeta}
is the closure of
{\mathds{N}}_{\upbeta}
in
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
, we have
\overline{{\mathds{N}}}_{\upbeta}=\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}_{\upbeta}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
, and by Lemma
3.10
and Proposition
5.4
, all
\overline{{\mathds{N}}}_{\upbeta}
are path connected.
Thus again by Proposition
5.4
, it suffices to show that
\overline{{\mathds{N}}}\cap\,\overline{\mathds{N}}_{\upgamma}=\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}\cap\,\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}_{\upgamma}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset
for any length one path
\upgamma
. If
\upgamma=s_{i}
, then consider the point
\upsigma=(Z,\mathcal{B})\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}
defined by
Z[\mathcal{S}_{i}]=-1/\uplambda_{i}
, and
Z[\mathcal{S}_{j}]=(1+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt)/n\uplambda_{j}
for all
j\neq i
, where
\uplambda_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{k}
. Then
\upsigma
lies in
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{B}\subset\overline{\mathbb{U}}\,\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
and in the codimension one boundary of
\operatorname{Stab}{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{B})
by
[
B5
, Lemma 5.5]
. But since
\operatorname{Stab}{\rm L}_{i}(\mathcal{B})=(\Upphi_{i})_{*}(\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{\upnu_{i}N})=\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{\upgamma}
by Lemma
5.5
,
\upsigma\in\overline{\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{B}_{\upgamma}}=\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{\upgamma}
. This implies that
\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}\cap\,\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}_{\upgamma}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset
. Similarly, we see that
\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}\cap\,\overline{{\mathbb{U}}}_{\upgamma}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}\neq\emptyset
when
\upgamma=s_{i}^{-1}
.
Hence
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
is connected, and thus
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
follows.
The remaining statements are then immediate from (
2
).
∎

6.2.
Regular Covering Structure

Lemma 6.5
.

If
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, respectively
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, then the following statements hold.

(1)

If a point
\upsigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}_{L}
is in
\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{\upalpha}
for some
\upalpha\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}}(C_{+})
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma)
is not on any complexified coordinate axis in
(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

If a point
\upsigma=(Z,\mathcal{B})\in\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}_{L}
is in
\overline{\mathbb{U}}_{\upbeta}
for some
\upbeta\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{+})
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma)
is not on any complexified coordinate axis in
(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

(1) First, for any
\uprho\in\mathrm{U}_{1}=\mathrm{U}
, every simple module
\mathcal{S}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{L}
is
\uprho
-semistable. Hence, by
[
BS
, 7.6]
,
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is
\uprho
-semistable for all
\uprho\in\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{1}
, and in particular
Z[\mathcal{S}_{i}]\neq 0
for all
(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{1}
.

Now, for
\upsigma\in\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{\upalpha}
, by Proposition
5.4
and Proposition
4.8
(
2
),
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma)=\mathcal{Z}_{L}((\Upphi_{\upalpha})_{*}^{-1}(\upsigma))
, and by definition
(\Upphi_{\upalpha})_{*}^{-1}(\upsigma)\in\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{1}
. Hence, by the first paragraph,
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma)
is not on any complexified coordinate axis.

(2) is identical to (1).
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

The map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}
restricts to a surjective map

\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}.

(2)

The map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
restricts to a surjective map

\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Proof.

(1) First, we show that
{\rm Im}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
. By Theorem
6.4
and Proposition
5.4
, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)=x+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty\in\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
for each point
\upsigma=(Z,\mathcal{A})\in\overline{\mathrm{U}}
in the closure of
\mathrm{U}
. Assume that
\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)\in H_{\mathbb{C}}
for some
H\in\mathcal{H}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{1\leq i\leq n\mid y_{i}=0\}
and
{\rm B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\upvartheta\in\Uptheta_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\upvartheta_{j}=0\mbox{ for all }j\in\mathcal{I}\}
. If
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, then the point
\upsigma
necessarily lies in
\mathrm{U}
, and so
\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)\in\mathbb{H}_{+}\subset\Uptheta\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Hence we may assume
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
. Since by Lemma
A.2
(
1
) the hyperplane
H
has the form
\uplambda_{1}\upvartheta_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}\upvartheta_{i_{s}}=0
for some
\uplambda_{1},\ldots,\uplambda_{s}>0
, the fact that
y_{j}>0
if
j\notin\mathcal{I}
implies
i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\in\mathcal{I}
. Since
{\rm B}\subseteq H
, by Lemma
A.8
there exist
k\in\mathcal{I}
and a minimal mutation sequence

\upalpha\colon L\to\ldots\to N\in\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{I}}(N)

such that
H=\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(H_{k})
, where
H_{k}=\{\upvartheta_{k}=0\}\subset(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{R}}
is the
k
th coordinate axis in
(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{R}}
.
If we set
\upsigma^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1})_{*}(\upsigma)\in\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}_{L}
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma^{\prime})\in(H_{k})_{\mathbb{C}}
. Since
y\in\overline{C}_{+}
and
\upalpha\in\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{I}}(N)
, we see that
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}(y)=y
by the rule (
3.B
), and so
{\rm Im}(\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma^{\prime}))\in\overline{C}_{+}
. But this implies that
\upsigma^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{U}}_{\upbeta}\subset\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}_{L}
for some
\upbeta\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}}(C_{+})
, which is a contradiction by Lemma
6.5
(
1
). Hence
{\rm Im}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Next, we show the map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
is surjective. Pick
z\in\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then by Proposition
3.8
there exists some
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
such that
\upvarphi_{L}(h)=z
for some
h\in\mathbb{H}_{+}
. The left hand side of the commutative diagram in Proposition
5.4
shows that we can find
\upsigma\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{A}_{L}
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma)=h
. The commutativity then shows that
\upsigma^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Upphi_{L})_{*}(\upsigma)\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{C}
maps, via
\mathcal{Z}
, to
z
. Since
\upsigma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
by Theorem
6.4
, it follows that
\mathcal{Z}
is surjective.

(2) By Theorem
6.4
and Proposition
5.4
, for
{\rm Im}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)=x+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
for any
\upsigma=(Z^{\prime},\mathcal{B})\in\overline{\mathds{N}}=\overline{\mathbb{U}}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}\kern-0.1pt\mathcal{D}
. To see this, let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{0\leq i\leq n\mid y_{i}=0\}
, and note that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subsetneq\{0,\ldots,n\}
since
x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
. If
\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, then by a similar argument to (1), now using Lemma
A.2
(
2
) and Lemma
A.8
(with
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
), there exists a minimal mutation sequence
\upalpha\colon L\to N\in\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}(N)
such that
\upsigma^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1})_{*}(\upsigma)
lies in
\mathbb{U}_{\upbeta}
for some
\upbeta\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{+})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{L}(\upsigma^{\prime})\in(H_{k})_{\mathbb{C}}\cap(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
for some coordinate axis
H_{k}
in
(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{R}}
. This contradicts Lemma
6.5
(
2
), and thus
{\rm Im}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
. The surjectivity of the map follows by a similar argument to (
1
), using Proposition
3.11
, Proposition
5.4
and Theorem
6.4
.
∎

Notation 6.7
.

Consider the subgroups of
\operatorname{Auteq}\mathcal{C}
and
\operatorname{Auteq}\mathcal{D}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Upphi_{\upalpha}|_{\mathcal{C}}\mid\upalpha\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}}(C_{+})\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Upphi_{\upbeta}|_{\mathcal{D}}\mid\upbeta\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{+})\}.

Theorem 6.8
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

The surjective map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
induces a homeomorphism

\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}/\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}.

(2)

The surjective map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}
induces a homeomorphism

\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}/\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Proof.

We only prove (
2
), since the proof of (
1
) is identical. Let
\upsigma\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
and
\Phi\in\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}(\Phi_{*}(\upsigma))=\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)
by Proposition
4.8
(
2
) and Proposition
5.4
, and so
\mathcal{Z}
induces a map
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}/\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
which is surjective by Lemma
6.6
.

We show that this induced map is injective.
Let
\upsigma,\upsigma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
be two points such that
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma)=\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma^{\prime})
. By Theorem
6.4
,
\upsigma\in\overline{\mathds{N}}_{\upbeta}
and
\upsigma^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathds{N}}_{\upbeta^{\prime}}
for some paths
\upbeta,\upbeta^{\prime}\in\mathop{\sf Term}\nolimits(C_{+})
. But by Proposition
3.11
, there is a unique
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
such that
x\in\upphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+})
, and so by Proposition
5.4
we see that
\upbeta,\upbeta^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{L},C_{+})
. Set
\upgamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upbeta^{\prime}\circ\upbeta^{-1}\in\operatorname{End}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{+})
, then by definition
(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\mathds{N}_{\upbeta})=\mathds{N}_{\upbeta^{\prime}}
.

Since the surjective map
\mathcal{Z}
is a local homeomorphism, there exists an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\upsigma
such that the restrictions
\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{Z}|_{(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\mathcal{U})}
are homeomorphisms. Choose a sequence
\{\upsigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathds{N}_{\upbeta}\cap\,\mathcal{U}
that converges to
\upsigma
, and set

\upsigma^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\upsigma_{i})\in\mathds{N}_{\upbeta^{\prime}}\cap(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\mathcal{U})
. Then again by Proposition
4.8
(
2
) and Proposition
5.4
, we have
x_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma_{i})=\mathcal{Z}(\upsigma_{i}^{\prime})
. The sequence
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
converges to
x
since
\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{U}}
is a homeomorphism. Moreover, since
\mathcal{Z}|_{(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\mathcal{U})}
is also a homeomorphism, the sequence
\{\upsigma_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
converges to
\upsigma^{\prime}
. Hence

\upsigma^{\prime}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\upsigma^{\prime}_{i}=\lim_{i\to\infty}(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\upsigma_{i})=(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\,\lim_{i\to\infty}\upsigma_{i}\,)=(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\upsigma).

This implies that
\upsigma=\upsigma^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}/\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
.
∎

Given a group
G
acting on a topological space
T
, consider the following condition.

(\ast)

For each
x\in T
, there is an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
x
such that
\mathcal{U}\cap g\mathcal{U}=\emptyset
for all
1\neq g\in G
.

Theorem 6.9
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}\to\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a regular covering map, with Galois group
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a regular covering map, with Galois group
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
.

Moreover, the covering map in (
1
) is universal.

Proof.

(2) We first show that the action of
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
on
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
satisfies the condition
(\ast)
. For this, take a point
\upsigma\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
and consider the open neighbourhood
\upsigma\in\mathcal{U}
defined by

\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\upsigma^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Stab}\mathcal{D}\mid d(\upsigma,\upsigma^{\prime})<1/4\}\cap\,\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D},

where
d(-,-)
is the metric introduced in
[
B4
, §6]
.

Assume that
\mathcal{U}\cap(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\mathcal{U})\neq\emptyset
for some
\Upphi_{\upbeta}\in\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
. Then, every point
\upsigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
must satisfy
d\bigl(\upsigma^{\prime},(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\upsigma^{\prime})\bigr)<1
. Furthermore, the central charges of
\upsigma^{\prime}
and
(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\upsigma^{\prime})
are equal by Proposition
4.8
(
2
) and Proposition
5.4
.
Therefore, it follows that
\upsigma^{\prime}=(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\upsigma^{\prime})
by
[
B4
, 6.4]
, for every
\upsigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.

By Theorem
6.4
, there is some
\mathds{N}_{\upgamma}
such that
\mathds{N}_{\upgamma}\cap\,\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset
, so choose
\uptau\in\mathds{N}_{\upgamma}\cap\mathcal{U}
. Then since
\uptau\in\mathds{N}_{\upgamma}
, the heart of
\uptau
is
(\Upphi_{\upgamma})_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{s(\upgamma)})
. But on the other hand, since
\uptau\in\mathcal{U}
, by the previous paragraph
\uptau=(\Upphi_{\upbeta})_{*}(\uptau)
. Thus the composition

\Upphi_{\upgamma^{-1}\upbeta\upgamma}=\Upphi_{\upgamma}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upbeta}\circ\Upphi_{\upgamma}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{s(\upbeta)})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{s(\upbeta)})

restricts to an equivalence on
\mathcal{B}_{s(\upgamma)}
. This implies
\Upphi_{\upgamma}^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upbeta}\circ\Upphi_{\upgamma}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
by Theorem
5.6
applied to
\upgamma^{-1}\upbeta\upgamma
. Thus
\Upphi_{\upbeta}\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
, and so the action satisfies the condition
(\ast)
.

Since
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
is path connected, as is standard
[
H1
, 1.40(a)(b)]
it follows that

\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
is the group of deck transformations for the regular cover

\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}/\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}.

Hence by Theorem
6.8
, the map
\mathcal{Z}\colon\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
is a regular covering map, with Galois group
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
. This completes the proof of (2).

(1) This follows using an identical argument to the above.

For the final statement, since
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
is a manifold, it is locally path connected. Hence as is standard (see e.g.
[
H1
, 1.40(c)]
) the cover is universal if and only if the natural map

\uppi_{1}(\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}

is injective. But this is
[
HW
]
, which works word-for-word in the more general terminal singularities setting here, as explained in
[
IW2
, §10.3]
.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
is contractible.

Proof.

The universal cover of the complexified complement simplicial hyperplane arrangement is contractible, due to Deligne’s work on the
K(\uppi,1)
conjecture
[
D1
]
.
∎

7.
Autoequivalence and SKMS Corollaries

The above description of stability conditions has consequences for autoequivalences, which in turn allows us to compute the SKMS.

7.1.
Autoequivalences of
\mathcal{C}

Consider the subgroup
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
of
\operatorname{Auteq}\mathcal{C}
, consisting of those
\Upphi|_{\mathcal{C}}
where
\Upphi
is a Fourier–Mukai equivalence
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
that commutes with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
and preserves
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
. Since
\Upphi
commutes with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
, automatically
\Upphi|_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose that
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is a 3-fold flop, where
X
has at worst terminal singularities. Then
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}\subset\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
follows since the Bridgeland–Chen flop functors are Fourier–Mukai equivalences that commute with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
, and
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}
acts on
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
by Theorem
6.9
.
For the reverse inclusion, consider
g\in\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
. Since
g\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
commutes with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
, passing through
\Uppsi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},-)
to obtain
\Uppsi\circ g\circ\Uppsi^{-1}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
, necessarily by
[
W1
, 2.14]

\Uppsi\circ g\circ\Uppsi^{-1}
commutes with the exact functor
e(-)
, where
e
is the idempotent of
\Lambda
corresponding to
R
.

Since
g
preserves
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{V}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Uppsi\circ g\circ\Uppsi^{-1}(\mathrm{U_{1}})
is open and by Theorem
6.4

\mathcal{M}
is dense in
\mathrm{Stab}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{C}
, necessarily
V\cap\mathcal{M}\neq\emptyset
. Thus we must have
\Uppsi\circ g\circ\Uppsi^{-1}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}_{\upalpha}
for some
\upalpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}}(C_{B},C_{+})
. Consider the composition

G=\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\circ\Uppsi\circ g\circ\Uppsi^{-1}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{B}),

which takes
\mathcal{A}
to the standard heart on
\mathcal{C}_{B}
. Now
\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}=\Upphi_{\upalpha^{-1}}
is a composition of mutation functors and their inverses, where we do not mutate the vertex
R
. By
[
W1
, 4.2]
these are functorially isomorphic to flop functors and their inverses, which commute with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
. Again by
[
W1
, 2.14]
, this translates into
\Upphi_{\upalpha^{-1}}
commuting with
e(-)
. Consequently the composition
G
commutes with
e(-)
.

Since
G
takes
\mathcal{A}
to a standard algebraic heart, necessarily
G
takes the simples
\mathcal{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{n}
to simples, a priori with a permutation. Hence (1)
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi
commutes with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
, and (2) it sends
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{1}}(-1),\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{n}}(-1)
to themselves, a priori up to permutation. But exactly as in
[
DW
, 7.17]
, property (1) implies that
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi
preserves
\mathcal{C}
, and property (2) implies that
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi
preserves the null category
\{a\in\operatorname{coh}X\mid\mathbf{R}f_{*}a=0\}
. This implies that it necessarily preserves
\mathcal{C}^{>0}
and
\mathcal{C}^{<0}
, and hence preserves zero perverse sheaves
{}^{0}\!\operatorname{Per}X
. In particular,
G(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cong\mathcal{S}_{0}
, as the other simples are permuted.

But since
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi
preserves
{}^{0}\!\operatorname{Per}X
,
G
restricts to a Morita equivalence
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda\to\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{B}
. In particular projectives map to projectives, so since
G(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cong\mathcal{S}_{0}
, under the pairing we have
G(\mathcal{P}_{0})\cong\mathcal{P}_{0}
. Furthermore, since
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{B}
are basic, the Morita equivalence sends
\Lambda\mapsto\Lambda_{B}
. Since
G
commutes with
e(-)
, it follows that
N\cong B
in
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}R)
, and so
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\in\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}
.

But now
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi
sends
\mathcal{O}_{X}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{X}
, since
G(\mathcal{P}_{0})\cong\mathcal{P}_{0}
, and it preserves
{}^{0}\!\operatorname{Per}X
. By the standard Toda argument (see e.g.
[
DW
, 7.18]
),
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi\cong\varphi_{*}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
for some isomorphism
\varphi\colon X\to X
and some line bundle
\mathcal{L}
. The line bundle
\mathcal{L}
is trivial since
\mathcal{O}_{X}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{X}
. The isomorphism
\varphi
commutes with
\mathbf{R}f_{*}
since
\Uppsi^{-1}\,\circ G\circ\,\Uppsi
does, and hence
\varphi
is the identity, given it must be the identity on the dense open set obtained by removing the flopping curve. It follows that
G\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
, and so
g=\Uppsi^{-1}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Uppsi\in\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{C}
, as required.
∎

7.2.
Identifying Line Bundle Twists

To describe
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
requires us to first realise twists by line bundles as compositions of mutation functors. Set
\mathsf{L}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{*}\mathcal{L}_{i}
and consider the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cong\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle\leq\mathop{\rm Cl}\nolimits(R)
. For any
\mathsf{L}
in this subgroup and any
M\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
,
\mathsf{L}\cdot M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathsf{L}\otimes M)^{**}
belongs to the mutation class of
N
, in the following predictable way.

Lemma 7.2
.

[
IW2
, 9.10(2)(3)]

The arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
has a
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
-action, where its generators take the chamber corresponding to
N
to the next chamber along the primitive vectors of the
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
-lattice given by the chamber
N
.

To illustrate this, consider the two-curve example in Figure
2
. Then the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-lattice is the black dots, and the primitive vectors of the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-lattice given by the chamber
N
are the red arrows.

\scriptstyle N
\scriptstyle\mathsf{L}_{\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0ptN
\scriptstyle\mathsf{L}_{\kern-0.5pt2}\kern-1.0ptN

Figure 2.
Example of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
action on
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}

For any such
\mathsf{L}
and
M\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, consider the isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda_{M}\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}\cdot M}
defined to be

\Lambda_{M}=\operatorname{End}_{R}(M)\xrightarrow{(-\otimes\mathsf{L})^{**}}\operatorname{End}_{R}((M\otimes\mathsf{L})^{**}).

Being an isomorphism of algebras,
\upvarepsilon
induces an isomorphism of categories
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{M})\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}\cdot M})
, which we will also denote by
\upvarepsilon
.

For
\mathsf{L}\in\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
and a path
\upalpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{M_{1}},C_{M_{2}})
, the translation by
\mathsf{L}
defines a path from
C_{\mathsf{L}\cdot M_{1}}
to
C_{\mathsf{L}\cdot M_{2}}
, which we denote by
\mathsf{L}\cdot\upalpha
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h}\in\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
,
M\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, and
\upalpha\colon C_{M}\to C_{\mathsf{g}\cdot M}
be a positive minimal path. Then the following diagram commutes.

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{M})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{g}\cdot M})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot M})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot\mathsf{g}\cdot M})
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
\Upphi_{\mathsf{h}\cdot\upalpha}
\upvarepsilon
\upvarepsilon

Proof.

By Remark
4.10
the top functor composed with the right hand functor is given by the tilting bimodule
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,\mathsf{g}\cdot M)
, with the natural action of
\Lambda_{M}
, and the action of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot\mathsf{g}\cdot M}
twisted by the isomorphism
\upvarepsilon
. Applying
\mathsf{h}\cdot
, this is clearly isomorphic, as bimodules, to
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\mathsf{h}\cdot M,\mathsf{h}\cdot\mathsf{g}\cdot M)
, with the action of
\Lambda_{M}
twisted by
\upvarepsilon
, and the natural action of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot\mathsf{g}\cdot M}
. But this is the bimodule that realises the left hand functor composed with the bottom functor, and so the diagram commutes.
∎

Theorem 7.4
.

Suppose that
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
a 3-fold flop, where
X
has at worst terminal singularities. Writing
\upkappa
for a minimal chain of mutation functors from one algebra to the other, for all
i=1,\ldots,n
the following diagram commutes

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}_{i}\cdot N})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\scriptstyle-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}^{*}
\scriptstyle\upkappa
\scriptstyle\upvarepsilon^{-1}
\scriptstyle\Uppsi
\scriptstyle\Uppsi

Proof.

By Remark
4.10
the minimal chain of mutations
\upkappa
is functorially isomorphic to the
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}
functor given by the tilting bimodule
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,\mathsf{L}_{i}\cdot N)
. So, setting
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{X}
and taking inverses, it suffices to prove that the diagram

(7.A)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

commutes, where derived tensors are over the relevant endomorphism ring.

The bottom composition is isomorphic to the functor

-\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}{}_{\upvarepsilon}\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}

by which we mean the
\Lambda\cong\operatorname{End}_{Y}(\mathcal{V})
-action on
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})
on the left is the composition action via
\upvarepsilon
, and the
\Lambda
-action on the right is the standard composition action. Hence the composition from bottom right to top left, along first the bottom and then the left, is functorially isomorphic to

(7.B)

-\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}({}_{\upvarepsilon}\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V})

We first claim that this bimodule has cohomology only in degree zero, and for this we can ignore the left action of
\Lambda
completely, and consider
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is generated by global sections, there exists a surjection
\mathcal{O}^{N}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i}
, for some
N
, and so tensoring by
\mathcal{V}
gives a surjection
\mathcal{V}^{N}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}
. Applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},-)
gives an exact sequence

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V})^{N}\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})\to 0

where
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}
is zero since flopping contractions have fibre dimension one. Since
\mathcal{V}
is tilting,
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V})=0
, thus
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})=0
. It follows that

(7.C)

\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}\cong\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}\cong\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i},

which has degree zero, as claimed.

By the claim, after truncating in the category of bimodules, (
7.B
) is functorially isomorphic to
-\otimes^{\bf L}_{\Lambda}({}_{\upvarepsilon}\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V})
. Thus (
7.A
) commutes provided that

(7.D)

{}_{\upvarepsilon}\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}\cong\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}

as
\Lambda
-
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-bimodules. But the derived counit map is an isomorphism, so by (
7.C
) it follows that the non-derived counit map

\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})_{\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits}\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}

is also an isomorphism. But this map is the obvious one. By inspection it respects the bimodule structure in (
7.D
), and thus it gives the required bimodule isomorphism.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

The position of
\upvarepsilon
at the end of the chain of mutations
\upkappa
in Theorem
7.4
is irrelevant. Indeed, given any minimal chain from
N
and
\mathsf{L}_{i}\cdot N
, consider any chamber
A
through which this minimal path passes. Abusing notation and writing
\upvarepsilon
for any isomorphism induced by the class group action, and
\upkappa
for a minimal composition of mutation functors, the following top diagram also commutes

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{A})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}_{i}^{-1}\cdot A})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}_{i}^{-1}\cdot N})
\scriptstyle-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}^{*}
\scriptstyle\upkappa
\scriptstyle\upvarepsilon^{-1}
\scriptstyle\upvarepsilon^{-1}
\scriptstyle\upkappa
\scriptstyle\upkappa
\scriptstyle\Uppsi
\scriptstyle\Uppsi

since the outer diagram commutes by Theorem
7.4
, and the commutativity of the bottom diagram is Lemma
7.3
. Hence, when composing line bundle twists, we can move all the
\upvarepsilon^{-1}
s to the right, or to the left, whichever is the most convenient.

7.3.
\operatorname{Pic}X
action

Recall that perfect complexes on
X
are equivalent to
\operatorname{K^{b}}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
, and
\operatorname{Pic}X\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with basis
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is the line bundle satisfying
\deg(\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{\mathrm{C}_{j}})=\delta_{ij}
. For
\mathcal{L}\in\operatorname{Pic}X
, by abuse of notation, set

-\otimes\mathcal{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Uppsi\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L})\circ\Uppsi^{-1}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda).

Lemma 7.6
.

Write
z=(z_{j})_{j=0}^{n}\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
with
z_{j}=x_{j}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty_{j}
. Then for
j>0
, and any
1\leq i\leq n
, the autoequivalence
-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}
acts on
\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
by

x_{j}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty_{j}\mapsto\begin{cases}x_{j}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt(y_{j}+1)&j=i\\
x_{j}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pty_{j}&j\neq i.\end{cases}

Proof.

To ease notation, consider only the action of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, since all other cases are identical. We first consider the action on the dual vector space
K_{0}(\mathcal{D})
of
\mathcal{K}
, with basis
[\mathcal{S}_{0}],\ldots,[\mathcal{S}_{n}]
. Recall that
\Uppsi(\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}[1])\cong\mathcal{S}_{0}
and
\Uppsi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1))\cong\mathcal{S}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Since
\deg(\mathcal{L}_{j}|_{\mathrm{C}_{i}})=\delta_{ij}
,

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}\cong\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{1}}&i=1\\
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)&i\neq 1.\end{cases}

Let
\mathcal{O}_{x}
be the skyscraper sheaf associated to a closed point
x\in\mathrm{C}
. Then
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}(\mathcal{V}_{i}^{*},\mathcal{O}_{x})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{i},\mathcal{O}_{x})=\mathbb{C}^{\operatorname{\sf rk}\kern-1.0ptN_{i}}
, so setting
\ell_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{i}
gives

(7.E)

[\mathcal{O}_{x}]=-[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)].

If
x\in\mathrm{C}_{i}
, then the exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}\to\mathcal{O}_{x}\to 0
implies that

[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)]+[\mathcal{O}_{x}]
. Combining this with (
7.E
), it follow that

(7.F)

[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}]=-[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}]+(\ell_{i}+1)[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)]+\sum_{k\neq i}\ell_{k}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{k}}(-1)],

and so

(7.G)

[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}]=\begin{cases}[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}[1]]+(\ell_{1}+1)[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{1}}(-1)]+\sum_{k\neq 1}\ell_{k}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{k}}(-1)]&i=1\\
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)]&i\neq j.\end{cases}

Next we determine the action of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
on
[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}[1]]=-[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}]
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{x}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}\cong\mathcal{O}_{x}
, we have

[\mathcal{O}_{x}]=-[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}]+\sum_{i\neq 1}\ell_{i}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)]+\ell_{1}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{1}}].

Substituting (
7.E
) to the left hand side and (
7.F
) to the third term of the right hand side,

(7.H)

[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}[1]\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1}]=(1-\ell_{1})[\upomega_{\mathrm{C}}[1]]-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}\ell_{1}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{C}_{i}}(-1)].

Combining (
7.G
) and (
7.H
), the action of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
on
K_{0}(\mathcal{D})
is given by the
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrix whose first row is
(1-\ell_{1},1,0,\ldots,0)
, whose second row is
(-\ell_{1}^{2},\ell_{1}+1,0,\ldots,0)
, and
(-\ell_{1}\ell_{j},\ell_{j},0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0)
is the
j
th row for any
j>1
, where
1
is in the
(j+1)
st entry.

The transpose of this matrix gives the required action on the dual
\mathcal{K}
. But for
j>0
, the transpose has
(j+1)
st row
(0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0)
where the only non-zero entry is in the
(j+1)
st position, and the first row of the transpose matrix is
(1,\ell_{1}+1,\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{n})
. Since
z\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
, by definition
x_{0}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}x_{i}
and
y_{0}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}y_{i}
. Using this, together with the rows identified, it is easy to check that the action is as stated.
∎

It will be convenient to visualise this in the case of an irreducible flopping contraction. Recall from Example
3.9
that
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
is determined by
(x_{1},y_{1})
, and that by Lemma
7.6
, the autoequivalence
-\otimes\mathcal{O}(1)
acts by sending
(x_{1},y_{1})\mapsto(x_{1},y_{1}+1)
. As in Example
3.9
, we draw the
y_{1}
axis horizontally pointing to the left, and the
x_{1}
axis vertically. Thus, with this convention, tensoring by
\mathcal{O}(1)
translates to the left. The case
\ell=3
is illustrated below.
