7.4.
Autoequivalences of
\mathcal{D}

As in the introduction, consider
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
, defined to be the subgroup of
\operatorname{Auteq}\mathcal{D}
consisting of those
\Upphi|_{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
where
\Upphi
is an
R
-linear Fourier–Mukai equivalence
\Upphi\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{coh}X)
that preserves
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
.

In order to control elements in
\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
, we first control isomorphisms of algebras that preserves the space of normalised stability conditions. Suppose that
\Lambda_{L}
and
\Lambda_{M}
arise from
L=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}
and
M=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}M_{i}
in
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, and

\uprho\colon\Lambda_{L}\to\Lambda_{M}

is an isomorphism of rings. Let
\mathcal{S}_{i}
be the simple
\Lambda_{L}
-module corresponding to the projective module
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(L,L_{i})
. Being an isomorphism, simples get sent to simples, and so there is a bijection
\upiota\colon\{0,\ldots,n\}\to\{0,\ldots,n\}
such that
\uprho_{*}\mathcal{S}_{i}\cong\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\upiota(i)}
, where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{j}
is the simple
\Lambda_{M}
-module corresponding to the summand
M_{j}
.

Lemma 7.7
.

\uprho_{*}
preserves
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
if and only if
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{i}=\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}M_{\upiota(i)}
for all
0\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) It suffices to show that
\uprho_{*}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{\Lambda_{L}})=(Z\circ\uprho^{-1},\mathcal{B}_{\Lambda_{M}})\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda_{M}}
for all
(Z,\mathcal{B}_{\Lambda_{L}})\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda_{L}}
. It is clear that
\uprho_{*}
preserves the stability component, since
Z\circ\uprho^{-1}[\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\upiota(i)}]=Z[\mathcal{S}_{i}]\in\mathbb{H}
for each
0\leq i\leq n
,
and, furthermore,
\uprho_{*}
preserves the normalisation since

\sum_{i=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}M_{\upiota(i)})\cdot Z\circ\uprho^{-1}[\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\upiota(i)}]=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{i})\cdot Z[\mathcal{S}_{i}]=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt.

(
\Rightarrow
)
To ease notation, set
a_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{i}
,
b_{j}=\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}M_{j}
. Furthermore, for each
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
consider
Z_{i}\colon K_{0}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

Z_{i}([\mathcal{S}_{j}])\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}-1&j\neq i\\
\frac{a-a_{i}}{a_{i}}+\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\frac{1}{a_{i}}&j=i\end{cases}

where
a\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}
. Now each
Z_{i}([\mathcal{S}_{j}])
belongs to
\mathbb{H}
, and
\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{j}Z_{i}([\mathcal{S}_{j}])=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt
, hence
(Z_{i},\mathcal{B}_{\Lambda_{L}})\in\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda_{L}}
. Since
\uprho_{*}
preserves normalised stability conditions by assumption,

\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{\upiota(j)}Z_{i}\circ\uprho^{-1}([\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\upiota(j)}])=\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_{\upiota(j)}Z_{i}([\mathcal{S}_{j}])=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt

Considering the imaginary parts of both sides, we see that

a_{i}=b_{\upiota(i)}.

∎

Our next main result, Theorem
7.10
, requires two technical lemmas.

Lemma 7.8
.

For any
\mathsf{g}\in\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
, consider the isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{g}\cdot N}
. Then there is an isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\cong\Upphi_{\upgamma}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
for some
\mathcal{L}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)
and some
\upgamma\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(N,\mathsf{g}\cdot N)
.

Proof.

By assumption we may write
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{L}_{i_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{i_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathsf{L}_{i_{k}}^{\sigma_{k}}
for some
k\geq 0
,
1\leq i_{j}\leq n
and
\sigma_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
. If
k=0
, we have nothing to prove, and so we assume
k>0
.

We prove the result by induction on
k
, so first assume that
k=1
. There are two cases, depending on the parity of
\sigma_{1}
. In the case
\sigma_{1}=1
, we are considering the isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}_{i}\cdot N}
. By Theorem
7.4
, the functor

(7.J)

(-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1})\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)

is isomorphic to the composition

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{\upalpha}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}_{i}\cdot N})\xrightarrow{\upvarepsilon^{-1}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)

from which it follows that
\upvarepsilon\cong\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i})
. On the other hand, in the case
\sigma_{1}=-1
, we are considering the isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}_{i}^{-1}\cdot N}
. However, by Theorem
7.4
and Lemma
7.3
applied to
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{L}_{i}
and
\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{g}^{-1}
, the functor (
7.J
) is isomorphic to

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\upvarepsilon}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot N})\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{\mathsf{h}\cdot\upalpha}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda),

from which it follows that
\upvarepsilon\cong\Upphi_{(\mathsf{h}\cdot\upalpha)^{-1}}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i}^{-1})
. Either way, the result holds for
k=1
.

For the induction step, assume that
k>1
and set
\mathsf{h}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{g}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{i_{k}}^{-\sigma_{k}}
and
\mathsf{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{L}_{i_{k}}^{\sigma_{k}}
, so
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{h}\cdot\mathsf{k}
. By the inductive hypothesis, there exist
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Pic}X
and
\upbeta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(N,\mathsf{h}\cdot N)
such that the isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot N}
is isomorphic to
\Upphi_{\upbeta}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{M})
. Combining this with Lemma
7.3
, the following diagram commutes

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{h}\cdot N})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{k}\cdot N})
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\mathsf{g}\cdot N})
-\otimes\mathcal{M}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,
\upvarepsilon
\Upphi_{\upbeta}
\Upphi_{\mathsf{k}\cdot\upbeta}
\upvarepsilon
\upvarepsilon
\upvarepsilon

where the right hand composition is our desired isomorphism
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{g}\cdot N}
. Now by length one case proved above applied to
\mathsf{k}
, the bottom left
\upvarepsilon
is isomorphic to
\Upphi_{\upgamma_{k}}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i_{k}}^{\sigma_{k}})
for some
\upgamma_{k}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(N,\mathsf{k}\cdot N)
.
Thus if we set
\upgamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathsf{k}\cdot\upbeta)\circ\upgamma_{k}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(N,\mathsf{g}\cdot N)
and
\mathcal{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{i_{k}}^{\sigma_{k}}
, then
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathsf{g}\cdot N}
is isomorphic to the composition
\Upphi_{\upgamma}\circ(-\otimes\mathcal{L})
.
∎

The following is an easy extension of results in
[
IW2
, §9]
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Suppose that
M\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, and that there is an
R
-linear isomorphism
\Lambda\to\Lambda_{M}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{0}\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{i}
, where
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}M_{i}=1
. Then
M\cong\mathsf{L}\cdot N
for some
\mathsf{L}\in\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{0}\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{i}
, by the pairing between simples and projectives, it follows that there is a chain of
R
-module isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,R)=\mathcal{P}_{0}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,M_{i}).

Since
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}M_{i}=1
, the right hand side is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M\cdot M_{i}^{-1},R)
. Hence applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(-,R)
shows that
M\cong M_{i}\cdot N
as
R
-modules. Since
M_{i}
is a summand of
M
of rank one, and
M\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, by the bijections in
3.3

M_{i}
must appear as a
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
-lattice point in the hyperplane arrangement (see
[
IW2
, 9.10(2)(3)]
).
∎

The following is one of our key results.

Theorem 7.10
.

\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\cong\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\rtimes\operatorname{Pic}X
.

Proof.

Set
G=\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
. Then
K=\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
is a clearly a subgroup of
G
, as is
H=\operatorname{Pic}X
since elements of
\operatorname{Pic}X
are
R
-linear by Lemma
2.5
, and preserve
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
by Theorem
7.4
.

The remainder of the proof splits into five steps.

Step 1
: We first claim that, as sets,
G=KH
. Let
g\in G
. Since
g
preserves
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
, arguing as in Theorem
7.1
, there exists
\upbeta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathds{G}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}(C_{M},C_{+})
such that
g(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{B}_{\upbeta}
. The composition

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upbeta})
\scriptstyle g
\scriptstyle\Upphi_{\upbeta}^{-1}

is
R
-linear, and restricts to an equivalence between finite length
\Lambda
-modules and finite length
\Lambda_{\upbeta}
-modules. In particular, by Lemma
5.7
(
1
) and Corollary
5.8
the composition restricts to an
R
-linear Morita equivalence

\operatorname{mod}\Lambda\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{\upbeta}^{-1}\circ g}\operatorname{mod}\Lambda_{\upbeta}.

Since both algebras are basic, necessarily this is induced by an
R
-algebra isomorphism
\upvarphi\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\upbeta}
. But by Lemma
7.7
, it follows that
M
has rank one summand. By Lemma
7.9
, there is
\mathsf{L}\in\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
such that
M\cong\mathsf{L}\cdot N
.

Consider the composition autoequivalence

where
\upvarepsilon\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda_{\mathsf{L}\cdot N}=\Lambda_{\upbeta}
. By a similar argument as above,
\Upupsilon
is induced by an
R
-linear isomorphism

\uppsi\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
. Then
\uppsi_{*}\mathcal{S}_{0}\cong\mathcal{S}_{i}
for some
0\leq i\leq n
, and by the pairing between projectives and simples,
\uppsi
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathcal{P}_{0}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i}
of
R
-modules. By Lemma
7.7
we see that
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{i}=1
. It follows that

\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N,R)=\mathcal{P}_{0}\cong\mathcal{P}_{i}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(N\cdot N_{i}^{-1},R)

as
R
-modules, and so dualizing gives
N\cong N\cdot N_{i}^{-1}
. Then by Lemma
7.9
,
N_{i}
necessarily lies in
\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
. By Lemma
7.2
we thus have
N_{i}\cong R
, since non-trivial elements of
\langle\mathsf{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{L}_{n}\rangle
non-trivially translate the hyperplane arrangement. Thus
\Upupsilon(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cong\mathcal{S}_{0}
. As an Morita equivalence, it follows that there exists a permutation
\upiota\in S_{n}
such that
\Upupsilon(\mathcal{S}_{i})\cong\mathcal{S}_{\upiota(i)}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
\Upupsilon
preserves normalised stability conditions, by Lemma
7.7
we see
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{i}=\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}N_{\upiota(i)}
. Hence by Proposition
5.10
, there is a functorial isomorphism
\Upupsilon\cong\mathop{\rm{Id}}\nolimits
, and so
g\cong\Upphi_{\upbeta}\circ\upvarepsilon
. Using Lemma
7.8
it follows that
g
is functorially isomorphic to the composition

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to314.37pt{\vbox to22.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.55528pt\lower-8.82802pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-23.22227pt}{-2.99501pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

for some
F\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)
. Hence
g\cong\Upphi_{\upbeta\circ\upgamma}\circ F\in KH
.

Step 2
: Consider the subgroup
\mathop{\sf Tr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
of
G
consisting of those elements that are the identity on K-theory
\mathcal{K}
. We know that
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathop{\sf Tr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
by Proposition
4.8
. We claim that
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\supseteq\mathop{\sf Tr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
, so equality holds. To see this, consider
t\in\mathop{\sf Tr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
. By Step 1, since
t\in G
we can write
t=kh
for some
k\in K
and some
h\in H
. Thus
h=k^{-1}t
, and so
h
is trivial on K-theory. But by Lemma
7.6
the only line bundle twist that satisfies this is the identity. Hence
h=1
, so
k=t
and thus
t\in K=\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
.

Step 3
:
K\unlhd\,G
. This follows immediately from Step 2, since being the identity on K-theory is clearly closed under conjugation.

Step 4
:
K\cap H=\{1_{G}\}
. Again, this holds by Lemma
7.6
, since the only line bundle twist that is the identity on K-theory is the identity.

Combining Steps 1, 3 and 4 we see that
G\cong K\rtimes H
, as required.
∎

In particular, as is standard for semidirect products, there is an induced exact sequence

(7.K)

1\to\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{Pic}X\to 1.

This sequence is the generalisation of
[
T
, 5.4(ii)]
to higher length flops.

7.5.
Application: Stringy Kähler Moduli Spaces

In this subsection we compute the stringy Kähler moduli space
\mathrm{Stab}_{n}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}/\mathrm{Aut}^{\kern-0.5pt\circ}\kern-0.2pt\mathcal{D}
for all smooth single curve flops. In this case, Theorem
3.3
reduces to the statement that the mutation class containing
N
is in bijection with the chambers of an infinite hyperplane arrangement in
\mathbb{R}^{1}
, which we draw as

extended in both directions to infinity. The walls are labelled by the indecomposable
R
-modules that are summands of elements in the mutation class of
N
, and the chambers are labelled by their direct sums. Wall crossing corresponds to mutation. Since
N=R\oplus N_{1}
from (
2.C
) must appear in its mutation class, the centre of the hyperplane arrangement has the following form

(7.L)

\scriptstyle N_{1}
\scriptstyle R
\scriptstyle N_{1}^{*}
R\oplus N_{1}
R\oplus N_{1}^{*}

Under this convention, with
R\oplus N_{1}
on the left and
R\oplus N_{1}^{*}
on the right,
\mathsf{L}=f_{*}\mathcal{L}=f_{*}\mathcal{O}(1)
generates a subgroup of
\mathop{\rm Cl}\nolimits(R)
which acts on (
7.L
), taking the wall labelled
R
to the next wall to the right for which the
R
-module labelling it has rank one.

The following computes the numerics of the hyperplane arrangements that can arise.

Proposition 7.11
.

Suppose that
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is an irreducible length
\ell
flop, where
X
is smooth. Then the corresponding affine hyperplane arrangement, together with the ranks of the modules labelling each wall, are, for
\ell=1,\ldots 6
respectively:

In each case the hyperplane arrangement is infinite, and the labels repeat.

Proof.

By Katz–Morrison
[
KM
,
K2
]
it is known that for a smooth single-curve flop, the
\mathcal{J}\subset\Updelta_{\kern 0.40974pt0}
is one of the following cases:

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to5.4pt{\vbox to5.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-4.4132pt\lower-2.7pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.3 0 C 13.3 1.91 11.75 3.46 9.84 3.46 C 7.93 3.46 6.38 1.91 6.38 0 C 6.38 -1.91 7.93 -3.46 9.84 -3.46 C 11.75 -3.46 13.3 -1.91 13.3 0 Z M 9.84 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

We analyse each individually. In each case, by
[
IW2
, §1]
the
\mathcal{J}
-affine arrangement
{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
can be calculated by using local wall crossing rules. Combinatorially, this is very elementary, and is explained in detail in
[
W2
, 1.1]
.
We sketch the
D_{4}
case here.

As in (
7.L
), consider the chamber

\scriptstyle R
\scriptstyle N_{1}^{*}

We first replace the modules by their ranks, and we label the chamber via McKay correspondence. The fact we will repeatedly use below is that the rank of a summand equals the number
\updelta_{i}
, where
i
is the vertex in the Dynkin diagram that the summand corresponds to, and
\updelta=(\updelta_{i})_{i\in\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
is the null root
[
IW2
, 9.3]
. Doing this, we obtain

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2

To obtain wall crossing over the wall labelled
2
, temporarily delete the vertex corresponding to
2
, apply the Dynkin involution to the
remainder
(which is trivial for
A_{1}\times A_{1}\times A_{1}\times A_{1}
), then insert back in the vertex labelled
2
.

delete
involution
insert

The wall crossing is thus described by

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1

Applying the same local rule but instead at the other vertex, and repeating, gives

\mathbb{R}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1

The following extends the pictures in
[
T
, p6169]
and
[
A
, Figure 1]
, which describe the case
\ell=1
, to all higher lengths.

Theorem 7.12
.

Suppose that
X\to\operatorname{Spec}R
is a length
\ell
irreducible flop, where
X
is smooth. Then the SKMS is one of the following:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to60.28pt{\vbox to57.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.13937pt\lower-28.67477pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}

Proof.

By Theorem
7.10
and the resulting exact sequence (
7.K
), we first quotient by
\mathop{\sf PBr}\nolimits\mathcal{D}
, then quotient by
\operatorname{Pic}X
. By Theorem
6.9
, it suffices to identify
(\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}})/\operatorname{Pic}X
.

But this is §
7.3
, see (
7.I
). Indeed, the action of
\mathcal{O}(1)
moves chambers to the left, by either
1,2,4,6,10,
or
12
steps, depending on the length of the flopping curve. Thus, for example in the case
\ell=2
, the generator
\mathcal{O}(-1)
of
\operatorname{Pic}X
acts via

where we have shaded the fundamental domain. Thus, identifying edges to form a cylinder, the quotient space is

All other cases are similar, by identifying the left and right hand sides of the fundamental regions shaded in the proof of Proposition

.
∎

Appendix A
Combinatorial Tracking Results

In this appendix, which is independent of the rest of the paper, we give the proof of Propositions
3.8
and
3.11
, and Lemma
3.10
. The Propositions are proved in §
A.2
, whilst Lemma
3.10
appears as Lemma
A.4
. Throughout, we use the notation from Section
3
.

A.1.
Preliminary Results

First, for
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
write
z\in(\Uptheta_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
as

z=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})+(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y}\,\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt.

The action of
\upvarphi_{L}
on K-theory is induced from
\mathbb{Z}
, so it independently acts on both factors
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y}
. We will write
\mathcal{H}_{x}
for the hyperplanes
\mathcal{H}
viewed in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{y}
for the hyperplanes
\mathcal{H}
viewed in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y}
. Likewise for
H\in\mathcal{H}
, we will write
H_{x}\in\mathcal{H}_{x}
and
H_{y}\in\mathcal{H}_{y}
accordingly. Since
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{H\times H\mid H\in\mathcal{H}\}
, by definition

(A.A)

z=x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\iff\exists\,H\in\mathcal{H}\mbox{ such that }x\in H_{x}\mbox{ and }y\in H_{y}.

On the other hand, for
L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
write
z\in(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
as

z=(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})+(y_{0},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{x}+\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{y}\,\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt.

The action of
\upphi_{L}
again acts independently on both factors. As in §
3.5
, consider
\mathcal{W}
, the set of full hyperplanes in
\mathcal{K}_{L}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}
that separate the open chambers of
{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
.

Example A.1
.

In Example
3.5
,
\mathcal{W}
is the following infinite collection of hyperplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

The hyperplanes converge on the line
\upvartheta_{0}+3\upvartheta_{1}=0
, but
\mathcal{W}
does not contain this line.

Write
\mathcal{W}_{x}
for the hyperplanes
\mathcal{W}
viewed in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{x}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{y}
for the hyperplanes
\mathcal{W}
viewed in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{y}
. Mirroring the above notation, for
W\in\mathcal{W}
, similarly consider
W_{x}\in\mathcal{W}_{x}
and
W_{y}\in\mathcal{W}_{y}
. Again, by definition

(A.B)

z=x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}\iff\exists\,W\in\mathcal{W}\mbox{ such that }x\in W_{x}\mbox{ and }y\in W_{y}.

The following is clear.

Lemma A.2
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

The hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}
contain the coordinate axes, and are all of the form
\uplambda_{1}x_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}x_{i_{s}}=0
where each
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and each
\uplambda_{j}>0
.

(2)

The hyperplanes in
\mathcal{W}
contain the coordinate axes, and are all of the form
\uplambda_{1}x_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}x_{i_{s}}=0
where each
i_{j}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and each
\uplambda_{j}>0
.

Proof.

By definition, in both cases
C_{+}
is a chamber. Since the coordinate axes bound this, both first statements follow. The second statements follow from the fact that
C_{+}
is a chamber, together with the observation that if some
\uplambda_{j}<0
, then the hyperplane would pass through
C_{+}
.
∎

The following is then immediate, and establishes
\supseteq
in Proposition
3.8
.

Corollary A.3
.

\bigcup\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\subseteq\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}

Proof.

By (
A.A
) it is clear that
\upvarphi_{L}
restricts to a bijection between
(\mathcal{H}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
, since we already know that it does this on both factors. Hence it suffices to show that
\mathbb{H}_{+}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

For this, consider
z=x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathbb{H}_{+}
. If all
y_{i}>0
, then by Lemma
A.2
(
1
), all
\uplambda_{1}y_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}y_{i_{s}}>0
, so
y\notin\mathcal{H}_{y}
, and hence
z\notin\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
by (
A.A
). By permuting the numbering if necessary, we can thus assume that
y_{1}=\ldots=y_{t}=0
for some
1\leq t\leq n
, and that
y_{t+1},\ldots,y_{n}>0
. In this case, by the positivity of
y_{t+1},\ldots,y_{n}
, and the fact the rest are zero, again using Lemma
A.2
(
1
) it follows that
y
avoids all members of
\mathcal{H}_{y}
except those hyperplanes of the form

\uplambda_{1}y_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}y_{i_{s}}=0

where
i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
. But since
z\in\mathbb{H}_{+}
, the fact that
y_{1}=\ldots=y_{t}=0
forces
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}<0
. Hence
x
avoids all the corresponding members

\uplambda_{1}x_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}x_{i_{s}}=0

of
\mathcal{H}_{x}
. Thus, overall,
y
avoids some hyperplanes, and the hyperplanes that it does not avoid are avoided by
x
. Again by (
A.A
) it follows that
z\notin\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

For the affine version of the above, recall that

\mathbb{E}_{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\biggr\{z\in\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\prime}\Bigm|\sum_{j=0}^{n}(\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{j})z_{j}=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\biggr\}=(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{+}.

The following is elementary.

Lemma A.4

(
3.10
)
.

The subspace
\mathbb{E}_{+}\subset(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
is path connected.

Proof.

Set
\uplambda_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}L_{i}
, and consider

\mathbb{E}_{+}^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\biggr\{z\in(\mathcal{K}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}\Bigm|\sum_{j=0}^{n}\uplambda_{j}z_{j}=\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\mbox{ and }\mathrm{Im}(z_{j})>0\mbox{ for all }j\biggr\}=\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\prime\circ}\cap(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}.

This is visibly path connected. To prove the statement, it suffices to show that for every boundary point
z\in\mathbb{E}_{+}\backslash\mathbb{E}_{+}^{\circ}
, there is a path in
\mathbb{E}_{+}
from
z
to a point
w
in
\mathbb{E}_{+}^{\circ}
.

Write
z=x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt
, then since
z\in\mathbb{E}_{+}
, not all
y_{j}
can be zero. Further, since
z\in\mathbb{E}_{+}\backslash\mathbb{E}_{+}^{\circ}
, after reordering if necessary we can write

y=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k},\underbrace{y_{k},\ldots,y_{n}}_{>0})\in\mathbb{H}_{+}^{\circ}\cap(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}.

for some
k
such that
0<k<n
. Set
\upgamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\uplambda_{i}
and fix
s
such that

0<s<\frac{\uplambda_{n}y_{n}}{\upgamma}
. Then for any
t\in[0,s]
, consider the point

y(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\underbrace{t,\ldots,t}_{k},y_{k},\ldots,y_{n-1},y_{n}-\tfrac{\upgamma t}{\uplambda_{n}})\in\mathcal{K}_{L}.

This satisfies
\sum_{i=0}^{n}\uplambda_{i}y(t)_{i}=\sum_{i=k}^{n}\uplambda_{i}y_{i}
, which equals
1
since
z\in\mathbb{E}_{+}
. Setting
z(t)=x+y(t)\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt
, it is then clear that
z(t)\in(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
for all
t\in[0,s]
. On the other hand, by inspection
z(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{+}
for all
t\in[0,s]
. It follows that
z(t)\in\mathbb{E}_{+}
for all
t\in[0,s]
, so setting
w\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x+y(s)\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathbb{E}_{+}^{\circ}
, the path
p\colon[0,s]\to\mathbb{E}_{+}
sending
t\mapsto z(t)
connects
z
and
w
.
∎

Recall that
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The following establishes
\supseteq
in Proposition
3.11
.

Corollary A.5
.

\bigcup\upphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+})\subseteq\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}

Proof.

By (
A.B
)
\upphi_{L}
restricts to a bijection between
(\mathcal{W}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Since mutation functors also preserve the level, and
\mathcal{H}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}_{+}\subseteq\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.

For this, consider
z=x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt\in\mathbb{E}_{+}
. The key is to view this in
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{+}
, then follow the proof of Corollary
A.3
. We appeal to Lemma
A.2
(
2
) instead of Lemma
A.2
(
1
), and (
A.B
) instead of (
A.A
), then it follows that
z\notin\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Hence
z\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
.
∎

To obtain the converse direction in both Propositions
3.8
and
3.11
is slightly more tricky. As preparation, recall that if
\mathcal{H}
is a real hyperplane arrangement, then the intersection poset
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
of
\mathcal{H}
is the set of all possible intersections of subsets of hyperplanes from
\mathcal{H}
. For
X,Y\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
, consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{X}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{H\mid X\subseteq H\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{Y}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{H\cap Y\mid Y\nsubseteq H\},

called the localization and restriction arrangements respectively. The localization
\mathcal{H}_{X}
is a subarrangement of
\mathcal{H}
, whilst
\mathcal{H}^{Y}
is an arrangement in
Y
. The following is elementary.

Lemma A.6
.

If
X\subseteq Y
, then
(\mathcal{H}_{X})^{Y}=(\mathcal{H}^{Y})_{X\cap Y}
.

Proof.

On one hand
(\mathcal{H}_{X})^{Y}=\{H\cap Y\mid X\subseteq H\mbox{ and }Y\nsubseteq H\}
. On the other hand

(\mathcal{H}^{Y})_{X\cap Y}=\{H\cap Y\mid Y\nsubseteq H\mbox{ and }X\cap Y\subseteq H\cap Y\}.

These two sets are clearly the same, using the assumption that
X\subseteq Y
.
∎

Returning to our flops setting, recall from Lemma
A.2
that the coordinate axes belong to
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{W}
. As notation, for a subset
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
, consider

\mathrm{B}=\{\upvartheta_{j}=0\mbox{ for all }j\in\mathcal{I}\}=\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{I}}\{\upvartheta_{i}=0\}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}).

Similarly, for
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, consider
\mathds{B}=\{x_{j}=0\mbox{ for all }j\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{W})
.
In the affine setting, the following result will be crucial.

Lemma A.7
.

If
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subsetneq\{0,\ldots,n\}
, then
\mathcal{W}_{\mathds{B}}
is finite

Proof.

Say the Tits cone associated to the extended ADE graph
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
lives inside the vector space
V\cong\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}|}
, with coordinates
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{m})
. Write
\mathcal{T}
for the set of full hyperplanes in
V
that separate the chambers in the Tits cone. The arrangement
\mathcal{W}
is the set of full hyperplanes that separate chambers in
{\sf TCone}{(\mathcal{J}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits})}
, so by Definition
3.1
we can write
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{T}^{Y}
for some
Y
obtained by intersecting coordinate axes. By the commutative diagram in Lemma
A.6
, to show that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathds{B}}=(\mathcal{T}^{Y})_{\mathds{B}}
is finite, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{T}_{X}
is finite for any
X=\bigcap_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\{x_{k}=0\}
with
\mathcal{K}\subsetneq\{0,\ldots,m\}
. In turn, it suffices to show that the quotient

\mathcal{T}/X\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{H/X\mid H\in\mathcal{T}_{X}\},

is a finite arrangement. This lives in
V/X
, which has lower dimension.

For
k\in\mathcal{K}
, applying the Coxeter element
s_{k}
to the basis
\{x_{j}+X\mid j\in\mathcal{K}\}
of
V/X
negates the
x_{k}
entry, and adds some multiple of
x_{k}
to its neighbours in
\mathcal{K}
, via the standard Coxeter rule. By inspection, this is the same as the Coxeter rule for
\Upgamma
, where
\Upgamma
is obtained from
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
by deleting the vertices that are not in
\mathcal{K}
. It follows that
\mathcal{T}/X
is the Tits cone associated to the diagram
\Gamma
. But deleting a non-empty set of vertices in an extended ADE Dynkin diagram gives a finite ADE Dynkin diagram, or a disjoint union thereof. Hence the Tits cone for
\Upgamma
has finitely many hyperplanes, hence so too does
\mathcal{T}/X
, and thus
\mathcal{T}_{X}
.
∎

Given
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subsetneq\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\upvartheta\in\Uptheta_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\upvartheta_{j}<0\mbox{ for all }j\in\mathcal{I}\}

\displaystyle\mathds{D}_{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{x\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{R}}\mid x_{j}<0\mbox{ for all }j\in{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\},

and let
\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{I}}(N)
, respectively
\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(N)
, be the set of all mutations
L\to\ldots\to N
whose constituent length one paths all have labels in the set
\mathcal{I}
, respectively
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. The following is one of the main technical results of this Appendix.

Proposition A.8
.

Consider subsets
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subsetneq\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, with associated
\mathrm{B}
and
\mathds{B}
. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

The hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{B}}
are precisely those hyperplanes
\uplambda_{1}\upvartheta_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}\upvartheta_{i_{s}}=0
from
\mathcal{H}
such that every
i_{j}\in\mathcal{I}
. Necessarily each
\uplambda_{j}>0
.

(2)

The hyperplanes in
\mathcal{W}_{\mathds{B}}
are precisely those hyperplanes
\uplambda_{1}x_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\uplambda_{s}x_{i_{s}}=0
from
\mathcal{W}
such that every
i_{j}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Necessarily each
\uplambda_{j}>0
.

(3)

There are decompositions

\bigcup_{\upalpha\in\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{I}}(N)}\kern-10.0pt\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(\mathrm{D}_{-})=\Uptheta_{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{B}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bigcup_{\upbeta\in\mathop{\sf MutTo}\nolimits_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(N)}\kern-10.0pt\upphi_{\upalpha}(\mathds{D}_{-})=\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\mathcal{W}_{\mathds{B}}.

Proof.

(1) The first statement is elementary. The second is immediate from Lemma
A.2
(
1
). Part (2) is identical, using instead Lemma
A.2
(
2
).

(3) Tracking across the mutation
\upnu_{i}N\to N
for
i\in\mathcal{I}
, by (
3.B
)
\mathrm{D}_{-}
gets sent to the region

(A.C)

\upvartheta_{i}>0,\quad\upvartheta_{j}+b_{ij}\upvartheta_{i}<0\mbox{ for }j\in\mathcal{I}-\{i\}

of
\Uptheta_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
b_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. In contrast,
C_{-}
gets sent to the region

(A.D)

\upvartheta_{i}>0,\quad\upvartheta_{j}+b_{ij}\upvartheta_{i}<0\mbox{ for }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}-\{i\}.

Thus, by part (
1
) we see that the walls of (
A.C
) are precisely those hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}
that belong to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{B}}
and also bound the walls of (
A.D
). Hence the region (
A.C
) is bounded by elements of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{B}}
. There are no further walls inside this region, since otherwise there would be further walls within (
A.D
), which is not the case, using the
C_{-}
version of Theorem
3.6
.

Repeating the above argument, all chambers adjacent to
\mathrm{D}_{-}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{B}}
can be obtained by tracking
\mathrm{D}_{-}
through some mutation
\upnu_{i}N\to N
. The proof then just proceeds by induction. Consider
\upnu_{j}\upnu_{i}N\to\upnu_{i}N\to N
, track
\mathrm{D}_{-}
through both mutations, and just appeal to the
C_{-}
version of Theorem
3.6
. This process finishes since the numbers of chambers in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{B}}
is finite, since
\mathcal{H}
is, and at each stage mutation at the labels in
\mathcal{I}
describes the
|\mathcal{I}|
possible wall-crossings in each chamber.

The last statement is similar, replacing
\Updelta
by
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
, and using the
C_{-}
version of Theorem
3.3
in place of Theorem
3.6
. The key point is that, since
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a proper subset, by Lemma
A.7
the hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{W}_{\mathds{B}}
is still finite, and so the conclusion of the last sentence in the above paragraph still holds.
∎

Corollary A.9
.

There is an inclusion
\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)}\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})
, and furthermore
\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}\subseteq\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)}\upphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+})
.

Proof.

Pick
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and write
z=x+y\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt
. By Theorem
3.6
applied to
\mathcal{H}_{y}
, we can find some
L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
such that
\upvarphi_{L}^{-1}(y)
has all coordinates
\geq 0
. If all coordinates are positive, then
\upvarphi_{L}^{-1}(z)\in\mathbb{H}_{+}
, thus
z\in\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})
, as required.

Hence we can assume that some coordinate of
y^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upvarphi_{L}^{-1}(y)\in\Uptheta_{L}
is zero. Thus there exists some non-empty subset
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
y^{\prime}_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, and
y^{\prime}_{j}>0
for
j\notin\mathcal{I}
. Note that
\mathcal{I}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
is possible, in which case all
y^{\prime}_{i}=0
.

Recall that
\upvarphi_{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upvarphi_{\upalpha}
, where
\upalpha\colon L\to\ldots\to N
. Write

\upvarphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}(z)=\upvarphi_{L}^{-1}(z)=x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt.

Since
x^{\prime}
belongs to the closure of some chamber in the
x
-version of
\Uptheta_{L}
, applying Proposition
A.8
(
3
) to
(\mathcal{H}_{L})_{x}\subset\Uptheta_{L}
we can find a sequence of mutations

M\xrightarrow{\upnu_{j_{1}}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\upnu_{j_{s}}}L

with each
j_{k}\in\mathcal{I}
, such that
\upvarphi_{j_{1}}^{-1}\ldots\upvarphi_{j_{s}}^{-1}(x)\in\overline{D}_{-}
. Since each
j_{k}\in\mathcal{I}
,
y^{\prime}_{j_{k}}=0
, and so this path has no effect on
y^{\prime}
. In particular,

(A.E)

\upvarphi_{j_{1}}^{-1}\ldots\upvarphi_{j_{s}}^{-1}\upvarphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}(z)=x^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime}\kern 1.0pt{\rm i}\kern 1.0pt,

where
x^{\prime\prime}_{i}\leq 0
provided that
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Since
z\notin\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we must have
x_{i}^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, else
\upvarphi_{j_{1}}^{-1}\ldots\upvarphi_{j_{s}}^{-1}\upvarphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}(z)
and thus
z
belongs to a complexified hyperplane. Hence (
A.E
) belongs to
\mathbb{H}_{+}
, and so
z\in\upvarphi_{\upalpha}\upvarphi_{j_{s}}\ldots\upvarphi_{j_{1}}(\mathbb{H}_{+})
. This is the tracking of
\mathbb{H}_{+}
through the chain

M\xrightarrow{\upnu_{j_{1}}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\upnu_{j_{s}}}L\overbrace{\to\ldots\to}^{\upalpha}N.

Appealing to Proposition
4.8
,
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}\upvarphi_{j_{s}}\ldots\upvarphi_{j_{1}}=\upvarphi_{M}
, and so
z\in\upvarphi_{M}(\mathbb{H}_{+})
.

For the second statement, let
z\in\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
. The proof proceeds as above, replacing
\upvarphi
by
\upphi
at all stages. We obtain a subset
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, but crucially now
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\neq\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
since
\upvarphi_{L}^{-1}(z)\in(\operatorname{\sf Level}_{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
. This is due to the fact that mutation functors preserve the level, and so multiples of the
y
coordinates must sum to one, hence not all the
y
can be zero. Hence
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a proper subset, which still allows us to still appeal to Proposition
A.8
(
3
). We thus still deduce that
\upphi_{M}^{-1}(z)\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{+}
. Since mutation functors preserve the level, automatically it follows that
\upphi_{M}^{-1}(z)\in\mathbb{E}_{+}
, and so
z\in\upphi_{M}(\mathbb{E}_{+})
.
∎

We lastly show that the unions are disjoint, which requires the following.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
L,M\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, respectively
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, and let
\upalpha\colon L\to M
be a minimal path. Suppose that
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{n})=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
in
(\Uptheta_{M})_{\mathbb{R}}
, respectively
\upphi_{\upalpha}(a^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{n})=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n})
in
(\mathcal{K}_{M})_{\mathbb{R}}
, with all
a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and write
\mathcal{I}=\{i\mid a^{\prime}_{i}=0\}
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, then
L\cong M
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, then the following statements hold.

(a)

The simple mutations giving
\upalpha
all have labels in the set
\mathcal{I}
.

(b)

a_{i}^{\prime}=a_{i}
for all
i
.

(c)

\upvarphi_{\upalpha}[\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}]=[\mathcal{P}_{i}]
, respectively
\upphi_{\upalpha}[\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}]=[\mathcal{P}_{i}]
, for all
i\notin\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

We prove the affine case
\mathcal{K}_{M}
, with the case
\Uptheta_{M}
being similar.

Set
p=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a^{\prime}_{i}[\mathcal{P}_{i}]
. For a general subset
J
of
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
N^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, set

C_{J}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}[\mathcal{P}_{i}]\in(\mathcal{K}_{N^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\begin{array}[]{cl}c_{i}=0&\mbox{if }i\in J\\
c_{i}>0&\mbox{if }i\notin J\end{array}\right.\right\}.

As calibration, note that
C_{+}=C_{\emptyset}
.

(1) If all
a_{i}>0
, then since chambers map to chambers,
\upphi_{\upalpha}(p)\in C_{+}
. Thus
C_{+}\cap\upphi_{\upalpha}(C_{+})\neq\emptyset
. By Theorem
3.3
(respectively
3.6
for
\Uptheta
),
L\cong M
.

(2) By assumption
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, in which case its complement
\mathcal{I}^{c}
in
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
is a proper subset. Since
p\in C_{\mathcal{I}}
, which is a codimension
|\mathcal{I}^{c}|
wall of
C_{+}
, and
\upphi_{\upalpha}
maps walls to walls (maintaining codimension), it follows that
\upphi_{\upalpha}(p)
lies in a codimension
|\mathcal{I}^{c}|
wall of
C_{+}
. These all have the form
C_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
for some
|\mathcal{I}^{\prime}|=|\mathcal{I}|
, and so it follows that
\upphi_{\upalpha}(p)\in C_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
for some such
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
with
|\mathcal{I}^{\prime}|=|\mathcal{I}|
.

We first argue that (a) implies (b) and (c). Indeed, since
\upphi_{j}
, with
j\in\mathcal{I}
, negates the entry
j
(which is zero) and adds zero to the neighbours, evidently this has no effect on elements in
C_{\mathcal{I}}
, and so
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n})=\upphi_{\upalpha}(a^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{n})=(a^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{n})
. Consequently, we have
a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}
for all
i
, proving (b). Furthermore, (c) follows immediately from (a), using Lemma
3.2
.

Now we prove (a). Write
\upalpha\colon L_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L\to L_{1}\to\ldots\to L_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M
for a minimal path from
L
to
M
. By
[
IW2
, 1.12]
, in every
\mathcal{K}_{L_{i}}
, all chambers and all walls of all codimension are labelled by Coxeter information. Namely, by
wC_{J}
for some
w
in the affine Weyl group
W_{\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
, and some subset
J
of the vertices of the affine Dynkin diagram
\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}
. The codimension
|\mathcal{I}^{c}|
walls have the form
wC_{J}
for certain
J
and
w\in W_{\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
, with
|J|=|\mathcal{I}^{c}|
. Consequently, tracking
C_{\mathcal{I}}
in
\mathcal{K}_{L}\otimes\mathbb{R}
through
\upphi_{\upalpha}=\upphi_{i_{n}}\ldots\upphi_{i_{1}}
gives a sequence of labelled walls

C_{\mathcal{I}}\mapsto w_{1}C_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\mapsto w_{2}C_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\mapsto\ldots\mapsto w_{m}C_{\mathcal{I}_{m}}=\upphi_{\upalpha}(C_{\mathcal{I}}),

where the last term is in
\mathcal{K}_{M}\otimes\mathbb{R}
. At each step, since atoms follow the weak order, the length of the smallest coset representative
w_{i}
cannot decrease. This holds just since the statement is true for chambers,
and the labels on the walls are induced from these.

Since
\upphi_{\upalpha}(p)\in C_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, and
\upphi_{\upalpha}(p)\in\upphi_{\upalpha}(C_{\mathcal{I}})=w_{m}C_{\mathcal{I}_{m}}
, it follows that
C_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\cap w_{m}C_{\mathcal{I}_{m}}\neq\emptyset
. As is standard
[
B2
, V.4.6, Proposition 5]
,
we deduce that
\mathcal{I}_{m}=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, and
w_{m}\in W_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, where
W_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
is the subgroup of
W_{\Updelta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}}
generated by
s_{i}
with
i\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. In particular
w_{m}C_{\mathcal{I}_{m}}=C_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
, and so the above chain is

C_{\mathcal{I}}\mapsto w_{1}C_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\mapsto w_{2}C_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\mapsto\ldots\mapsto C_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}.

As the minimal length of the coset representative
w_{i}
cannot decrease throughout the chain, and the chain starts and finishes with length zero, it follows that at each step
w_{i}C_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}=C_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
. Thus, at step one,
\upphi_{i_{1}}\colon C_{\mathcal{I}}\mapsto C_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}
. By inspection, this occurs if and only if the label
i_{1}
is in the set
\mathcal{I}
, and
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{1}
. Inducting along the chain, every label
i_{t}
is in the set
\mathcal{I}
, and (a) follows.
∎

Corollary A.11
.

Suppose that
L,M\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, and let
\upalpha
be the minimal path from
L
to
M
. Then

\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\cap\mathbb{H}_{+}\neq\emptyset
in
\Uptheta_{M}\iff L\cong M
. The same statement holds for
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, using instead
\upphi_{\upalpha}(\mathbb{E}_{+})\cap\mathbb{E}_{+}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) is clear. For (
\Rightarrow
), if the intersection is nonempty, then

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(z^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{n})=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

where all
z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{H}
. Splitting into real and imaginary parts,

(A.F)

\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n})=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,y^{\prime}_{n})=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}).

Since all
z_{i},z_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}
, necessarily the right hand equation belongs to
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(\overline{C}_{+})\cap\overline{C}_{+}
.
As before, write
\mathcal{I}
for the set of
i
for which
y^{\prime}_{i}=0
.

On one hand, if
\mathcal{I}
is not a proper subset, then
y_{i}=y^{\prime}_{i}=0
for all
i
. Since every
z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{H}
, all
x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}<0
. Using the
C_{-}
version of Proposition
3.6
applied to the
x
-coordinate,
L\cong M
. On the other hand, if
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, then by Lemma
A.10
(
1
) we also have
L\cong M
.

Hence we can assume that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{I}
is a proper subset. Since
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, by Lemma
A.10
(
2
),
\upalpha
comprises of mutations with labels only from the set
\mathcal{I}
. Further, all
y_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i}
(and so in particular
y_{i}=0
if
i\in\mathcal{I}
), and
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}[\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}]=[\mathcal{P}_{i}]
if
i\notin\mathcal{I}
. Since
z_{i},z_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}
, we then deduce that
x_{i}<0
and
x_{i}^{\prime}<0
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, and that we can re-write the left hand equation in (
A.F
) to obtain

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}x_{i}^{\prime}\upvarphi_{\upalpha}[\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}]+\sum_{i\notin\mathcal{I}}x_{i}^{\prime}[\mathcal{P}_{i}]=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}x_{i}[\mathcal{P}_{i}]+\sum_{i\notin\mathcal{I}}x_{i}[\mathcal{P}_{i}].

Set
I\subset\mathcal{I}^{c}
to consist of those
i
such that
x^{\prime}_{i}-x_{i}\geq 0
, and let
I^{c}=\mathcal{I}^{c}-I
. Re-arranging gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}(x^{\prime}_{i}-x_{i})[\mathcal{P}_{i}]+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(-x_{i})[\mathcal{P}_{i}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I^{c}}(x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i})[\mathcal{P}_{i}]-\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}x_{i}^{\prime}\upvarphi_{\upalpha}[\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i}].

\displaystyle=\upvarphi_{\upalpha}\left(\sum_{i\in I^{c}}(x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i})[\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}]+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(-x_{i}^{\prime})[\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}]\right).

The left hand side has non-negative coefficients in every entry
\{0,1\ldots,n\}
, and the right hand side is in
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(\overline{C}_{+})
. If
I=\emptyset
, so that
I^{c}=\mathcal{I}^{c}
, then the right hand side is in
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(C_{+})
and so by Lemma
A.10
(
1
),
L\cong M
.

Hence our final case is when
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
,
\mathcal{I}
is proper, and
I\neq\emptyset
. We will show that this cannot occur, by exhibiting a contradiction. Again, the above displayed equation lies in
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(\overline{C}_{+})\cap\overline{C}_{+}
and so now by Lemma
A.10
(
2
) the coefficients on both sides must match. But coefficients in
I^{c}
do not appear on the left hand side, nor do coefficients in
I
on the right, so we deduce that
x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}^{c}
. But then

\upvarphi_{\upalpha}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(-x^{\prime}_{i})[\mathcal{P}_{i}]\right)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(-x_{i})[\mathcal{P}_{i}],

and so by Lemma
A.10
(
2
)
\upalpha
comprises mutations only from the set
\mathcal{I}^{c}
. But as stated above,
\upalpha
can only comprise labels in the set
\mathcal{I}
. This is a contradiction, which shows that this final case cannot exist.

The proof of the second statement is similar. The set
\mathcal{I}
must be proper since
z\in\mathbb{E}_{+}
, so all
y
coordinates are nonnegative, and after weighting by
\operatorname{\sf rk}_{R}M_{i}
they sum to one. Hence not all can be zero. Given this, the rest of the proof remains the same: since
\mathbb{E}_{+}\subset\mathbb{H}^{\prime}_{+}
, replacing
\upvarphi
by
\upphi
throughout, and starting the indices with
0
, the logic above still holds, as we can still appeal to Lemma
A.10
in the affine case.
∎

Corollary A.12
.

If
L,M\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)
, then
\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\cap\upvarphi_{M}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\neq\emptyset
in
\Uptheta_{N}\iff L\cong M
. The same statement holds for
\operatorname{Mut}(N)
, using instead
\upphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+})\cap\upphi_{M}(\mathbb{E}_{+})\neq\emptyset
in
\mathcal{K}_{N}
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
) is clear. For (
\Rightarrow
), if
\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\cap\upvarphi_{M}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\neq\emptyset
, then
\upvarphi_{M}^{-1}\circ\upvarphi_{L}^{\phantom{-}}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\cap\mathbb{H}_{+}\neq\emptyset
. By Proposition
4.8
(
3
),
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(\mathbb{H}_{+})\cap\mathbb{H}_{+}\neq\emptyset
in
\Uptheta_{M}
, where
\upalpha
is the minimal path from
L
to
M
. By Corollary
A.11
, the atom
\upalpha
is the identity, and the result follows. The second statement is identical, as we can still appeal to Proposition
4.8
(
3
) and Corollary
A.11
.
∎

A.2.
Proof of Propositions
3.8
and
3.11

Proposition
3.8
asserts that there is an equality

\Uptheta_{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}_{0}(N)}\upvarphi_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{+}),

where the union on the right hand side is disjoint. This now follows by combining Corollaries
A.3
,
A.9
and
A.12
. On the other hand, Proposition
3.11
asserts that

\operatorname{\sf Level}_{\mathbb{C}}\!\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\operatorname{\mathsf{aff}}\nolimits}=\bigcup_{L\in\operatorname{Mut}(N)}\upphi_{L}(\mathbb{E}_{+}),

where the union on the right hand side is disjoint. This now follows by combining Corollaries
A.5
,
A.9
and
A.12
.
